└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: The Observability domain covers understanding different log types. You must distinguish application logs (debugging) from audit logs (security/compliance). While Prometheus itself doesn't do audit logging, understanding the observability landscape includes knowing where each log type fits.

Observability Q15 of 209
What factors determine log retention policies, and what is a typical retention strategy for anihpj/jobpost — balancing debugging needs, compliance requirements, and storage costs?
A) Logs should be kept forever — storage is cheap and you never know when you'll need old logs
B) Retention balances 3 factors: debugging (keep recent logs for incident response — 7-30 days), compliance (keep audit logs per regulation — 1-7 years), and cost (tier storage: hot SSD for recent, cold S3 for archive). anihpj: 30 days hot (Loki), 90 days warm (S3), 1 year audit logs
C) Retention is purely about disk space — delete logs when the disk is 80% full regardless of age or type
D) Logs older than 24 hours are useless — delete everything daily to save costs
✅ Correct Answer: B

Log retention is a three-way tradeoff: Debugging needs (you need recent logs for incident response — most incidents are detected within hours, rarely need logs older than 30 days for debugging), Compliance requirements (SOC2, HIPAA, PCI-DSS mandate keeping audit logs for 1-7 years), and Storage costs (hot SSD storage is expensive per GB/month; cold S3/Blob storage is ~10x cheaper). The solution: tiered retention — hot storage for recent logs (7-30 days for fast querying), warm/cold storage for older logs (90 days to 7 years on cheaper object storage).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Log Retention Strategy — Tiered Storage for anihpj
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  RETENTION TIER       AGE         STORAGE     QUERY SPEED  COST  │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  🔥 HOT (Loki SSD)    0–30 days   Local SSD    < 1 second   $$$ │
│     → Active debugging, incident response, daily queries         │
│     → Example: "What caused yesterday's outage?"                 │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  🌤️ WARM (Loki S3)   30–90 days   Object Store  2–5 seconds  $$ │
│     → Trend analysis, post-mortems, capacity planning            │
│     → Example: "Is error rate increasing over the last 2 months?" │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  ❄️ COLD (S3 Glacier) 90d–7 years  Archive       minutes      $ │
│     → Compliance audits, legal holds, forensic investigations    │
│     → Example: "SOC2 auditor needs last year's access logs"     │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  APPLICATION LOGS:  30d hot + 90d warm  → debugging priority     │
│  AUDIT LOGS:        30d hot + 7yr cold  → compliance priority    │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 Prometheus metrics retention: typically 15-30 days local     │
│  💡 Long-term metrics: use Thanos/Cortex with S3                 │
│  💡 Logs are larger than metrics — plan 5-10× more storage       │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: The Observability domain covers understanding data lifecycle. You must know that Prometheus has its own retention (--storage.tsdb.retention.time), logs have different retention tiers, and these are separate concerns. Also, Prometheus metrics ≠ logs — they have different retention strategies.

1.3 Tracing & SpansQ16-Q22

Observability Q16 of 209
What is a distributed trace, and how does it differ from a single log line or a metric data point?
A) A trace is a log line with a trace_id field — it's the same as structured logging
B) A trace is a Prometheus metric with a trace_id label — it's stored in the TSDB
C) A trace represents the complete journey of a single request across multiple services — composed of spans (each service's work), connected by a trace_id. Unlike a single log (one event) or metric (one number), a trace shows the full causal chain: user → web → api → database → response
D) A trace is a type of Prometheus histogram that tracks request duration across services
✅ Correct Answer: C

A distributed trace is the complete recorded journey of a single request as it travels through multiple services. It's composed of spans — each span represents one service's work (a database query, an HTTP call, a function execution). All spans share a trace_id. Unlike a single log line (one event at one service) or a single metric (one aggregated number), a trace shows causality: "The API was slow because the database query took 2.3 seconds, which was caused by a missing index on the jobs table." For anihpj: a trace follows a job application from the web tier → API → PostgreSQL → Redis → response.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Trace Anatomy — One Request Through anihpj/jobpost
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  TRACE: user clicks "Apply for Job #456"                          │
│  trace_id: a1b2c3d4e5f6                                          │
│                                                                  │
│  ┌─ SPAN 1: anihpj-web (200ms) ──────────────────────────────┐  │
│  │  span_id: s1                                               │  │
│  │  GET /jobs/456 → render template → POST /api/jobs/apply    │  │
│  │  ┌─ SPAN 2: anihpj-api (180ms) ───────────────────────┐   │  │
│  │  │  span_id: s2  parent: s1                            │   │  │
│  │  │  Validate request → Check auth → Process application│   │  │
│  │  │  ┌─ SPAN 3: PostgreSQL (150ms) ────────────────┐   │   │  │
│  │  │  │  span_id: s3  parent: s2                    │   │   │  │
│  │  │  │  SELECT user, job → INSERT application      │   │   │  │
│  │  │  │  → SLOW! Missing index on applications      │   │   │  │
│  │  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │   │  │
│  │  │  ┌─ SPAN 4: Redis (2ms) ─────────────────────┐   │   │  │
│  │  │  │  span_id: s4  parent: s2                   │   │   │  │
│  │  │  │  GET job:456:applications_count → INCR     │   │   │  │
│  │  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │   │  │
│  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │  │
│  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 Trace reveals: bottleneck is PostgreSQL (150ms of 200ms)     │
│  💡 Metrics would show: p99 latency = 200ms (but not WHY)       │
│  💡 Logs would show: "INSERT took 150ms" (but not WHERE in flow)│
│  💡 Traces show: the CAUSAL CHAIN — this span caused that delay  │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: The Observability domain covers understanding traces as the third pillar. You must know that traces ≠ metrics ≠ logs, and that traces provide the causal chain that neither metrics nor logs can show alone.

Observability Q17 of 209
What is a span in distributed tracing, and what core information does every span contain?
A) A span is a single unit of work within a trace — it contains: span_id, trace_id, parent_span_id, operation name, start timestamp, duration, and optional tags/logs. Spans form a tree with parent-child relationships
B) A span is the time between two log entries. It contains the difference in timestamps
C) A span is a Prometheus Counter that measures the duration of HTTP requests
D) A span is a single log line with a trace_id field — just structured logging
✅ Correct Answer: A

A span is the fundamental building block of a trace — it represents a single unit of work (a function call, an HTTP request, a database query). Every span has: span_id (unique identifier for this span), trace_id (shared across all spans in the same request), parent_span_id (which span called this one — null for root span), operation name ("POST /api/jobs/apply", "SELECT users"), start timestamp + duration (when it started and how long it took), and optional tags (http.status_code=200, db.statement="SELECT...") and events/logs (timestamped annotations within the span).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Span Anatomy — What's Inside Every Span
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  SPAN: "POST /api/jobs/apply"                                     │
│                                                                  │
│  span_id:        "s2"                   ← unique per span         │
│  trace_id:       "a1b2c3d4e5f6"        ← shared across all spans │
│  parent_span_id: "s1"                   ← who called me?          │
│  operation_name: "POST /api/jobs/apply" ← what am I doing?       │
│  start_time:     1718387535.123        ← when did I start?       │
│  duration:       180ms                  ← how long did I take?    │
│                                                                  │
│  TAGS (key-value metadata):                                       │
│    http.method:      "POST"                                       │
│    http.url:         "/api/jobs/apply"                             │
│    http.status_code: 200                                          │
│    service.name:     "anihpj-api"                                 │
│    user_id:          "12345"                                      │
│                                                                  │
│  EVENTS (timestamped within span):                                │
│    [0ms]   "Request parsed"                                       │
│    [5ms]   "Authentication checked"                               │
│    [10ms]  "SELECT user FROM users WHERE id=12345" → 150ms       │
│    [165ms] "INSERT INTO applications VALUES(...)" → 13ms         │
│    [178ms] "Response sent"                                        │
│                                                                  │
│  STATUS: OK / ERROR                                               │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 Parent-child links create the TRACE TREE                      │
│  💡 Tags = indexed/searchable; Events = chronological log         │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Understanding spans is essential for grasping how the three pillars complement each other. Spans provide the structured, causal data that connects metrics (WHAT) and logs (WHY) into a complete picture (WHERE in the call chain).

Observability Q18 of 209
How does trace context propagation work, and what HTTP headers carry trace information between anihpj services?
A) Each service generates its own random trace_id — they're correlated later by timestamp matching
B) Trace context is stored in a shared Redis cache — each service reads/writes the current trace context
C) Trace context is sent via Kubernetes labels on the pod — the next service reads pod metadata
D) Trace context (trace_id, span_id, sampling decision) is propagated via HTTP headers — typically W3C TraceContext headers (traceparent, tracestate). The first service generates the trace_id; each downstream service reads the headers and creates child spans with parent_span_id pointing to the caller
✅ Correct Answer: D

Trace context propagation is how trace information travels across service boundaries. When anihpj-web calls anihpj-api, it injects HTTP headers (W3C TraceContext: traceparent: 00-a1b2c3d4e5f6-s2-01) into the outgoing request. The API reads these headers, knows it's part of trace a1b2c3d4e5f6 with parent span s2, and creates its own span (s3) with parent_span_id=s2. This chain continues through every service call. Without propagation, each service would create unrelated traces — you'd lose the causal chain.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Trace Context Propagation — W3C TraceContext Headers
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                                                                      │
│  anihpj-web                        anihpj-api                        │
│  ┌──────────────┐   HTTP Request   ┌──────────────┐                  │
│  │ trace_id:    │                  │ trace_id:    │                  │
│  │  a1b2c3...   │  traceparent:    │  a1b2c3...   │ ← READ from     │
│  │              │  00-a1b2c3...-   │              │   header        │
│  │ span_id: s1  │  s1-01           │ span_id: s2  │ ← CREATE new    │
│  │              │ ────────────────→ │              │   child span    │
│  │ parent: null │                  │ parent: s1   │ ← points to     │
│  │              │                  │              │   caller's span │
│  └──────────────┘                  └──────┬───────┘                  │
│                                          │                           │
│                                          │ HTTP Request              │
│                                          │ traceparent:              │
│                                          │  00-a1b2c3...-            │
│                                          │  s2-01                    │
│                                          ↓                           │
│                                   ┌──────────────┐                  │
│                                   │ PostgreSQL   │                  │
│                                   │ trace_id:    │ ← READ           │
│                                   │  a1b2c3...   │                  │
│                                   │ span_id: s3  │ ← CREATE         │
│                                   │ parent: s2   │ ← from API       │
│                                   └──────────────┘                  │
│                                                                      │
│  W3C traceparent header format:                                      │
│    00-a1b2c3d4e5f6789012345678901234-s1234567890123456-01            │
│    ^^ ^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^ ^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^ ^^          │
│    ver trace_id (32 hex)               span_id (16 hex)   flags      │
│                                                                      │
│  💡 Without propagation: 3 separate traces — no causal link          │
│  💡 With propagation: 1 trace with 3 spans — complete visibility     │
│  💡 OpenTelemetry standardizes this across ALL languages             │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: The Observability domain covers traces, and trace context propagation is the mechanism that makes distributed tracing work. You must understand that headers carry the context, not side channels or shared storage.

Observability Q19 of 209
How do traces complement metrics and logs to form the "three pillars of observability"? Which scenario best demonstrates all three working together?
A) Metrics show request count, logs show request bodies, traces show response times — they're independent views of the same data
B) Metrics DETECT: "p99 latency jumped from 200ms to 2s (WHAT)." Logs EXPLAIN: "NullPointerException in PaymentService (WHY)." Traces LOCATE: "The payment API call to Stripe timed out after 30s, blocking the entire request chain (WHERE)." Together they provide complete incident triage
C) You only need one of the three — metrics alone are sufficient for complete observability if you have enough dashboards
D) The three pillars are: Prometheus (metrics), Grafana (dashboards), and Alertmanager (alerts) — logs and traces are optional additions
✅ Correct Answer: B

The three pillars work in a detection → investigation → localization pattern. Metrics (Prometheus) are your always-on radar — they detect anomalies cheaply at scale ("latency is high"). Logs (Loki) are your investigation tool — they provide detailed context once an anomaly is detected ("NullPointerException with this stack trace"). Traces (Tempo) are your localization tool — they show the exact service, operation, and code path causing the issue ("the Stripe API call in PaymentService is the bottleneck"). Together: Prometheus alerts → click through to Loki logs → follow trace_id to Tempo → find exact root cause.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Three Pillars Working Together — anihpj Incident Response
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                                                                      │
│  ⏰ 10:32:00 — INCIDENT BEGINS                                       │
│                                                                      │
│  1️⃣ METRICS (Prometheus) — DETECT                                    │
│     Alert fires: "anihpj-api p99 latency > 2s for 5 minutes"        │
│     PromQL: histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(http_request_duration_     │
│              seconds_bucket{job="anihpj-api"}[5m])) > 2             │
│     → WHAT: API is slow. Users are affected.                         │
│                                                                      │
│  2️⃣ LOGS (Loki) — EXPLAIN                                            │
│     Query: {service="anihpj-api", level="ERROR"} [5m]                │
│     Result: "10:32:14 ERROR PaymentService.java:89                   │
│              PaymentGatewayTimeout: Stripe API timeout after 30s"    │
│     trace_id in log: a1b2c3d4e5f6                                    │
│     → WHY: Stripe payment gateway is timing out.                     │
│                                                                      │
│  3️⃣ TRACES (Tempo) — LOCATE                                          │
│     Query: trace_id = a1b2c3d4e5f6                                   │
│     Result: 6 spans. Span "POST /api/jobs/apply" = 30.2 seconds.    │
│              Child span "POST https://api.stripe.com/charge" = 30s.  │
│              All other spans < 50ms.                                 │
│     → WHERE: The Stripe API call is the bottleneck — it consumed     │
│        99% of the total request time.                                │
│                                                                      │
│  4️⃣ RESOLUTION                                                       │
│     Root cause: Stripe API degraded.                                  │
│     Fix: Enable circuit breaker to fail fast instead of waiting 30s. │
│     Update: Add Stripe API latency to Prometheus alerts.             │
│                                                                      │
│  💡 Metrics = WHAT (detection)    → always on, cheap                 │
│  💡 Logs = WHY (explanation)      → detailed, searchable              │
│  💡 Traces = WHERE (localization)  → causal chain, pinpoint           │
│  💡 Together = complete incident response in minutes, not hours       │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: This is the core concept of the Observability Concepts domain. You must understand that Prometheus handles metrics (pillar 1), NOT logs or traces, and that all three pillars are necessary for complete observability.

Observability Q20 of 209
In anihpj/jobpost, a user reports that clicking "Apply for Job" takes 10 seconds. Using distributed tracing, how would you identify which service is causing the delay?
A) Check each service's CPU usage in Grafana — the one with the highest CPU is the bottleneck
B) Look at each service's log file and find the one with the most ERROR-level logs
C) Open the trace for that request in Tempo/Jaeger — the waterfall view shows each span's duration. The span with the largest bar (longest duration) is the bottleneck. In anihpj, you'd see: web=50ms, api=9500ms, db=9000ms → the database query is the bottleneck
D) Run kubectl top pod on all anihpj pods and see which one has the highest memory usage
✅ Correct Answer: C

Distributed tracing provides a waterfall view that visualizes every span in a request as a horizontal bar — the width = duration. You can instantly see which span is the widest (longest duration). In the anihpj example: the "SELECT applications JOIN jobs" query takes 9 seconds out of 10 — so you know to investigate the database (missing index? table scan?). Without tracing, you'd have to manually instrument timing code in every service, guess based on CPU/memory, or grep through logs. Tracing gives you the answer in one view.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Waterfall View — Identifying the Bottleneck in 10s anihpj Request
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  TRACE: "Apply for Job #456" — Total: 10,050ms                        │
│                                                                      │
│  SPAN                           DURATION (ms)    PERCENTAGE          │
│  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
│  anihpj-web: GET /jobs/456      ██ 50ms            0.5%  ← Fast!    │
│  └─ anihpj-api: POST /apply     █████████████████ 10000ms  99.5%     │
│    ├─ Auth: validate_token      █ 15ms             0.1%  ← Fast!    │
│    ├─ DB: SELECT applications   █████████████████ 9000ms  89.6% ←!!!│
│    │  JOIN jobs ON...           █████████████████                    │
│    ├─ DB: INSERT application    █ 10ms             0.1%  ← Fast!    │
│    └─ Redis: INCR counter       █ 2ms              0.02% ← Fast!    │
│                                                                      │
│  🔍 ANALYSIS:                                                        │
│     The "SELECT applications JOIN jobs" query takes 9000ms out of    │
│     10000ms total — this is 89.6% of the entire request.             │
│                                                                      │
│  🔧 FIX:                                                             │
│     Check: EXPLAIN SELECT ... → "Seq Scan on applications"           │
│     Root cause: Missing index on applications.user_id                │
│     Fix: CREATE INDEX idx_applications_user_id ON applications(...)  │
│     Result: Query drops from 9000ms → 12ms (750× faster)            │
│                                                                      │
│  💡 Without tracing: guess which service is slow (hours)             │
│  💡 With tracing: point to exact query in seconds                    │
│  💡 Tracing + metrics: confirm fix (p99 drops from 10s → 200ms)     │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: The Observability domain tests your ability to apply observability tools to real scenarios. Tracing is the tool for latency root-cause analysis — metrics tell you latency is high, traces show you exactly WHERE.

Observability Q21 of 209
What is sampling in distributed tracing, and why can't you trace 100% of requests in a high-traffic system like anihpj/jobpost?
A) Sampling means only tracing errors — successful requests don't need tracing
B) Sampling selects a subset of requests to trace because tracing 100% is prohibitively expensive — each span consumes memory, network, and storage. At 1000 req/sec, 100% sampling = 1000 traces/sec × ~5 spans each = 5000 spans/sec → massive overhead. Head sampling decides at request start; tail sampling decides after the request completes and can prioritize errors/slow requests
C) Sampling is only needed for Prometheus — traces are cheap and can always be 100%
D) Sampling means taking a screenshot of the trace — it's a UI feature, not a data collection strategy
✅ Correct Answer: B

Sampling is essential because tracing 100% of requests at scale is impossible — each span consumes CPU (creating span objects), memory (buffering), network (sending to collector), and storage (saving). At 1000 req/sec with 5 spans each = 5000 spans/sec = 432 million spans/day. At ~500 bytes/span = 216 GB/day just for trace data. Head sampling (probabilistic — trace 1% of all requests) is simple but misses rare errors. Tail sampling (decision made after request completes) can keep 100% of errors and slow requests while sampling normal ones — ideal for anihpj where you always want to see failed job applications.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Head vs Tail Sampling — When Trace Decisions Are Made
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  HEAD SAMPLING (decision at request START)                        │
│                                                                  │
│  Request arrives → random(1-100) → if ≤ 10: trace it             │
│  ✅ Simple, low overhead                                          │
│  ❌ May miss rare but important requests (the 1 error in 10,000) │
│  ❌ Can't decide based on result (did it error? was it slow?)    │
│                                                                  │
│  TAIL SAMPLING (decision at request END)                          │
│                                                                  │
│  Request arrives → ALWAYS start tracing (lightweight)            │
│  Request completes → evaluate:                                    │
│    • Was there an ERROR?     → KEEP (100% of errors!)            │
│    • Was latency > 500ms?    → KEEP (all slow requests!)         │
│    • Was it a normal request? → SAMPLE (keep 1%)                 │
│  ✅ Keeps ALL interesting traces (errors, slow)                  │
│  ✅ Drops boring traces (normal, fast)                           │
│  ❌ Slightly more overhead (must start tracing every request)    │
│                                                                  │
│  anihpj strategy: Tail sampling with Tempo                        │
│    • Keep 100% of ERROR traces + traces > 500ms                  │
│    • Keep 5% of normal traces for baseline                       │
│    • Storage: ~50GB/day for 1000 req/sec (vs 216GB for 100%)    │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 Metrics (Prometheus): always 100% — cheap aggregation        │
│  💡 Traces: sampled — expensive per-request data                 │
│  💡 Logs: can be sampled at high volume, full at lower volume    │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Understanding sampling is critical — the PCA exam tests why traces are sampled (cost) and how this differs from metrics (always 100% — cheap aggregation). Know the difference between head and tail sampling.

Observability Q22 of 209
What is OpenTelemetry, and why is it significant for the Prometheus/observability ecosystem?
A) OpenTelemetry (OTel) is a CNCF project providing a unified standard for generating, collecting, and exporting telemetry data (traces, metrics, logs). It's significant because it decouples instrumentation from backends — you instrument ONCE with OTel libraries and can send data to Prometheus, Tempo, Loki, or any other backend without changing application code
B) OpenTelemetry is a Prometheus replacement that stores metrics more efficiently. It replaces Prometheus, Grafana, and Loki with a single binary
C) OpenTelemetry is a Grafana plugin that adds trace visualization to Prometheus dashboards
D) OpenTelemetry is a security scanning tool that checks Prometheus configurations for vulnerabilities
✅ Correct Answer: A

OpenTelemetry (OTel) is a CNCF incubating project that provides a vendor-neutral standard for telemetry. Instead of using Prometheus client libraries for metrics, a separate tracing library for traces, and a logging library for logs — you use OTel SDKs that generate ALL telemetry types through ONE API. The OTel Collector can then export to any backend: Prometheus (metrics), Tempo/Jaeger (traces), Loki/Elasticsearch (logs). For anihpj/jobpost, this means instrumenting Django ONCE with OTel Python SDK → exporting metrics to Prometheus AND traces to Tempo without changing code per-backend.

📝 Detailed Explanation
OpenTelemetry — One Instrumentation, Any Backend
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  WITHOUT OpenTelemetry (vendor lock-in):                          │
│                                                                  │
│  Django app                                                      │
│    ├─ prometheus_client (for Prometheus metrics)                 │
│    ├─ jaeger-client (for Jaeger traces)                          │
│    ├─ python-json-logger (for Loki logs)                         │
│    └─ 3 different APIs, 3 different configs                      │
│                                                                  │
│  WITH OpenTelemetry (one standard):                               │
│                                                                  │
│  Django app                                                      │
│    └─ opentelemetry-api + opentelemetry-sdk (ONE API)            │
│         │                                                        │
│         ↓  OTel Collector (can be sidecar or DaemonSet)          │
│         │                                                        │
│         ├─→ Prometheus  (metrics via OTLP → Prometheus)         │
│         ├─→ Tempo        (traces via OTLP → Tempo)              │
│         └─→ Loki         (logs via OTLP → Loki)                 │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 OTel = "write once, export anywhere"                         │
│  💡 CNCF project — industry standard (AWS, GCP, Azure support)   │
│  💡 Prometheus supports OTLP natively (v2.40+)                   │
│  💡 PCA exam covers OTel as part of observability landscape     │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: OpenTelemetry is part of the modern observability landscape tested on the PCA exam. You must know that OTel is a standard for instrumentation, NOT a replacement for Prometheus — Prometheus is still the metrics backend, OTel is how you GET metrics into Prometheus.

1.4 Push vs PullQ23-Q29

Observability Q23 of 209
What is the fundamental difference between the Pull model and Push model for metrics collection, and which does Prometheus use?
A) Pull = applications send metrics to Prometheus. Push = Prometheus requests metrics from applications. Prometheus uses Push
B) Pull = Prometheus actively scrapes (HTTP GET) /metrics endpoints on a schedule. Push = applications actively send metrics to a collector. Prometheus uses Pull — the scraper initiates the connection to each target
C) Pull and Push are the same thing — the direction doesn't matter because HTTP is bidirectional
D) Pull = metrics are downloaded from cloud storage. Push = metrics are uploaded to cloud storage. Prometheus doesn't use either — it uses a message queue
✅ Correct Answer: B

Prometheus uses the Pull model: the Prometheus server initiates HTTP connections to each target's /metrics endpoint on a scrape_interval (default 15s). The target doesn't know about Prometheus — it just exposes /metrics. Benefits: Prometheus controls scrape frequency, can detect DOWN targets (failed scrape), works with service discovery, and doesn't require targets to know about Prometheus. The Push model (used by Graphite, InfluxDB, StatsD) has applications actively send metrics to a collector — better for short-lived jobs but requires targets to know about the collector.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Pull vs Push — How Prometheus Scrapes anihpj
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  PULL MODEL (Prometheus)                                          │
│                                                                  │
│  Prometheus Server                                                │
│  ┌────────────────────┐                                          │
│  │ scrape_config:     │    HTTP GET /metrics (every 15s)         │
│  │  - job: anihpj-api │────────────────────────────→ ┌─────────┐ │
│  │    targets:        │                              │ anihpj-  │ │
│  │      anihpj-api:80 │←──────────────────────────── │ api pod  │ │
│  │                    │    HTTP 200 + metrics text    │ :8000    │ │
│  └────────────────────┘                              └─────────┘ │
│                                                                  │
│  ✅ Prometheus controls WHEN to scrape (scrape_interval)          │
│  ✅ Prometheus knows target is UP/DOWN (failed scrape = DOWN)    │
│  ✅ Target just exposes /metrics — no config needed              │
│  ✅ Works with service discovery (K8s SD, DNS SD, etc.)          │
│                                                                  │
│  PUSH MODEL (Graphite/InfluxDB style)                             │
│                                                                  │
│  App ──POST /write──→ Collector ──→ Database                     │
│                                                                  │
│  ✅ Good for short-lived jobs (cronjobs, batch processes)        │
│  ❌ App must know about collector address                        │
│  ❌ Can't detect DOWN targets (no scrape to fail)                │
│  ❌ Potential for data loss if collector is down                 │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 Prometheus also supports Push via Pushgateway (special case) │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Pull vs Push is a fundamental concept. Prometheus is pull-based by design. The Pushgateway is the exception (for batch jobs), not the rule. You'll be tested on when to use each.

Observability Q24 of 209
When should you use the Prometheus Pushgateway, and what are its critical limitations?
A) Use Pushgateway for ALL metrics — it's the recommended way to get data into Prometheus. No limitations
B) Use Pushgateway for long-running services that can't expose /metrics. It provides high availability and automatic cleanup of old metrics
C) Use Pushgateway ONLY for short-lived/ephemeral jobs (cronjobs, batch processes) that can't be scraped because they exit before Prometheus can reach them. Limitations: metrics persist until manually deleted (no TTL), no HA (single point of failure), cannot track job duration, and pushed metrics become stale if the job stops pushing without cleanup
D) Use Pushgateway as a permanent metrics cache for all services — it buffers metrics when Prometheus is down
✅ Correct Answer: C

The Pushgateway is a special-purpose workaround for jobs that are too short-lived to be scraped (lifespan < scrape_interval). For anihpj, the daily "Generate Job Report" cronjob runs for 30 seconds then exits — Prometheus scrapes every 15s but might miss it. Solution: cronjob pushes metrics to Pushgateway at completion, then Prometheus scrapes the Pushgateway. Critical limitations: pushed metrics persist forever until manually deleted via API (no TTL), the Pushgateway is a single point of failure, you lose the ability to track job duration (you only see the final state), and nothing stops multiple jobs from pushing conflicting values.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Pushgateway — When (and When NOT) to Use It
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  ✅ USE Pushgateway:                                              │
│    • CronJobs / batch processes (lifespan < scrape_interval)     │
│    • One-shot data processing pipelines                          │
│    • anihpj: daily report generation → push "reports_generated"  │
│                                                                  │
│  ❌ DO NOT USE Pushgateway:                                       │
│    • Long-running services (use /metrics endpoint instead!)      │
│    • Counters from multiple instances (conflicting values)       │
│    • As a permanent metrics store (no TTL, no HA)               │
│    • For high-frequency metrics (becomes bottleneck)             │
│                                                                  │
│  ⚠️ LIMITATIONS:                                                 │
│    • Metrics persist FOREVER (no automatic cleanup)              │
│    • Single point of failure (no clustering)                     │
│    • No timestamps (Prometheus adds scrape timestamp)            │
│    • Security: anyone can push metrics (no auth by default)     │
│    • Can't distinguish "job completed" from "job crashed"        │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 The PCA exam tests: "Should you use Pushgateway for a web    │
│     server?" → NO. "For a daily batch job?" → YES.              │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: The Pushgateway is one of the most commonly MISUNDERSTOOD Prometheus components. The PCA exam WILL test that you know it's ONLY for short-lived jobs, not for services.

Observability Q25 of 209
What are the key tradeoffs between Pull-based and Push-based monitoring — covering freshness, discovery, security, and scalability?
A) Pull: Prometheus controls scrape schedule (predictable load), service discovery auto-finds targets, targets don't need to know about Prometheus (simpler apps), firewall only needs outbound from Prometheus. Push: apps control when to send (better for bursty/sporadic work), works through NAT/firewalls without exposing ports, but requires apps to know about collector address and collector must handle spikes
B) Pull has no advantages — Push is superior in every way and Prometheus should switch to it
C) Push and Pull have identical tradeoffs — the choice is purely aesthetic preference
D) Pull is only used by Prometheus; Push is used by every other monitoring system. Prometheus is the exception, not the rule
✅ Correct Answer: A

Pull and Push each have distinct tradeoffs. Pull advantages: Prometheus controls scrape frequency (predictable load on targets), service discovery integrates naturally (Prometheus discovers targets, not vice versa), targets stay simple (just expose /metrics), security is simpler (only need outbound from Prometheus, not inbound to apps). Push advantages: works for short-lived jobs, works through NAT/firewalls (app initiates connection outbound), apps control timing (good for bursty/sporadic work). The PCA exam tests your understanding of these tradeoffs — why Prometheus chose Pull, and when Push (Pushgateway) is the right exception.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Pull vs Push Tradeoffs — Decision Matrix
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  CRITERIA          PULL (Prometheus)       PUSH (Pushgateway)    │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Freshness         Predictable (every Ns)   App decides when     │
│  Target discovery  Built-in (SD)            Manual (app knows)   │
│  Security          Only outbound from Prom  App opens connection │
│  Firewall/NAT      Harder (targets must     Easier (app connects │
│                    expose /metrics port)    outbound)            │
│  App complexity    Simple (just /metrics)   Must know collector  │
│  Short-lived jobs  ❌ Missed if job ends     ✅ Pushes before exit│
│  Load control      Prometheus controls      App controls         │
│  HA               Run 2 Prometheus          Single Pushgateway   │
│  Duplicate data   Each Prom scrapes all     Multiple pushers=dup │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 Prometheus = Pull-first design                               │
│  💡 Pushgateway = exception, not the rule                        │
│  💡 Know the tradeoffs for the PCA exam                          │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Understanding why Prometheus chose Pull and when Push is appropriate is a core PCA concept. You'll be tested on both the philosophy (Pull is the default) and the exception (Pushgateway for batch jobs).

Observability Q26 of 209
The anihpj/jobpost application has a daily cronjob that generates a job recommendation report, runs for 20 seconds, and exits. How should you monitor this job's success/failure in Prometheus?
A) Expose a /metrics endpoint on the cronjob pod — Prometheus will scrape it within the 15s interval
B) Write metrics to a log file and have Fluentd parse them into Prometheus metrics
C) Use Prometheus Blackbox exporter to probe the cronjob's health check endpoint
D) Use the Pushgateway: the cronjob pushes a metric like reports_generated_total{status="success"} 1 to the Pushgateway at the end of its run, then Prometheus scrapes the Pushgateway. This is the textbook use case for Pushgateway — a short-lived batch job that exits before Prometheus can scrape it
✅ Correct Answer: D

This is the textbook Pushgateway use case. The anihpj report cronjob runs for 20 seconds — shorter than a typical scrape_interval (15-30s). Even if Prometheus scrapes during the 20s window, there's a race condition. Solution: when the cronjob finishes (success or failure), it pushes a metric to the Pushgateway: echo "reports_generated_total{status=\"success\"} 1" | curl --data-binary @- http://pushgateway:9091/metrics/job/report_cron. Prometheus scrapes the Pushgateway every 15s. This guarantees the metric is captured regardless of timing.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj Report Cronjob — Pushgateway Monitoring Flow
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  CRONJOB: "Generate Daily Job Recommendations" (runs 20s)        │
│                                                                  │
│  ┌─────────┐     ┌─────────┐     ┌──────────────┐               │
│  │ CronJob │────→│  Runs   │────→│ ON SUCCESS:  │               │
│  │ triggers│     │  report │     │ push metric   │               │
│  │ at 2AM  │     │  gen    │     │ to Pushgateway│               │
│  └─────────┘     │ (20 sec)│     └──────┬───────┘               │
│                  └─────────┘            │                        │
│                                         ↓                        │
│                              ┌──────────────────┐                │
│                              │   PUSHGATEWAY     │                │
│                              │   :9091/metrics   │                │
│                              │                   │                │
│                              │ reports_generated_│                │
│                              │ total{status=      │                │
│                              │ "success"} 1       │                │
│                              └────────┬─────────┘                │
│                                       │                          │
│                                       │ scrape every 15s         │
│                                       ↓                          │
│                              ┌──────────────────┐                │
│                              │   PROMETHEUS      │                │
│                              │   scrapes PG      │                │
│                              └──────────────────┘                │
│                                                                  │
│  ALERT: reports_generated_total{status="success"} == 0           │
│         → "Daily report did NOT run today!"                      │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 This is THE textbook Pushgateway example for PCA             │
│  💡 Note: MUST manually clean up old metrics from Pushgateway    │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: This is the canonical Pushgateway scenario. The exam will present a short-lived job and ask how to monitor it — the answer is Pushgateway. Know it cold.

Observability Q27 of 209
What happens to metrics in the Pushgateway if the pushing job crashes and never sends a final "success" or "failure" metric?
A) The Pushgateway automatically detects the crash and marks metrics as stale after 5 minutes
B) The Pushgateway keeps the LAST pushed value forever — it has no TTL or staleness detection. If the job crashed without pushing, the old metric value from the PREVIOUS successful run remains, creating a false positive: Prometheus still sees the old "success" metric and won't alert that today's job failed
C) The Pushgateway deletes all metrics for the job when it detects the connection was lost
D) Prometheus automatically marks Pushgateway metrics as stale after 5 minutes because it can't scrape them directly
✅ Correct Answer: B

This is the biggest Pushgateway trap: metrics in the Pushgateway persist forever with no TTL. If the anihpj report cronjob crashes before pushing, the Pushgateway still shows yesterday's reports_generated_total{status="success"} 1 — Prometheus scrapes it, sees "success", and your alert doesn't fire. You falsely believe the report ran when it didn't. Mitigation: always push a timestamp or use a separate heartbeat/timestamp metric, and consider using push_time_seconds (automatically set by Pushgateway) to detect stale values.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Pushgateway Staleness Problem — Silent Failure
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  DAY 1: Cronjob runs successfully                                │
│    → pushes reports_generated_total{status="success"} 1          │
│    → Prometheus sees "success = 1" ✅                            │
│                                                                  │
│  DAY 2: Cronjob CRASHES before pushing                           │
│    → Pushgateway STILL has reports_generated_total=1 from Day 1  │
│    → Prometheus STILL sees "success = 1" ← FALSE POSITIVE!      │
│    → Alert does NOT fire because it still sees the old value     │
│    → Nobody knows the report failed!                             │
│                                                                  │
│  SOLUTION: use push_time_seconds                                 │
│    Pushgateway auto-adds: push_time_seconds{job="report_cron"}   │
│    Alert: time() - push_time_seconds{job="report_cron"} > 86400  │
│    → "Report hasn't been pushed in 24 hours!"                    │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 Pushgateway = NO staleness, NO TTL, NO automatic cleanup     │
│  💡 Always add a timestamp-based staleness check                 │
│  💡 Better: use a metrics-generating sidecar instead             │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: The PCA exam tests Pushgateway limitations. The staleness problem is the #1 trap — know that metrics persist forever and you must add your own staleness check.

Observability Q28 of 209
What is the recommended Prometheus-native alternative to Pushgateway for monitoring batch jobs in Kubernetes, and why is it better?
A) Use a sidecar container that exposes a /metrics endpoint — the sidecar runs alongside the batch job, collects its metrics, and Prometheus scrapes the sidecar. Better because: (1) metrics have TTL tied to pod lifecycle, (2) no Pushgateway SPOF, (3) Prometheus can detect pod DOWN
B) Use two Pushgateways for high availability — it solves all the limitations
C) Write metrics to a PersistentVolume and have Prometheus mount the same volume
D) Increase scrape_interval to 1 second so Prometheus never misses short-lived jobs
✅ Correct Answer: A

The sidecar pattern is the modern alternative to Pushgateway for Kubernetes batch jobs. A metrics sidecar runs alongside the batch job container in the same pod, collecting metrics (via shared emptyDir volume or localhost). Prometheus scrapes the sidecar's /metrics endpoint. When the pod terminates, the metrics disappear — no stale data problem. This gives you all the benefits of Pull (scrape detection, service discovery, TTL via pod lifecycle) without the Pushgateway's limitations (no staleness, no SPOF, no manual cleanup). For anihpj's report cronjob, a sidecar can track job start time, duration, and exit status.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Sidecar Pattern — Better Than Pushgateway for K8s Batch Jobs
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  PUSHGATEWAY APPROACH (old):                                      │
│    CronJob pod → push metrics → Pushgateway ← scrape ← Prometheus│
│    ❌ Stale data, SPOF, no TTL                                   │
│                                                                  │
│  SIDECAR APPROACH (modern):                                       │
│    ┌─────────────────────────┐                                   │
│    │ CronJob Pod             │                                   │
│    │ ┌─────────┐ ┌─────────┐ │    scrape /metrics                │
│    │ │ Batch   │ │ Metrics │ │←────────────────── Prometheus     │
│    │ │ Job     │ │ Sidecar │ │                                   │
│    │ │ (20s)   │ │ :9090   │ │    Metrics die with pod — no      │
│    │ └─────────┘ └─────────┘ │    stale data!                     │
│    └─────────────────────────┘                                   │
│                                                                  │
│  ✅ Pod lifecycle = automatic TTL for metrics                    │
│  ✅ Pod DOWN = Prometheus detects via failed scrape              │
│  ✅ No SPOF (each pod has its own sidecar)                      │
│  ✅ Works with standard K8s service discovery                    │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 Sidecar is the recommended pattern for K8s batch monitoring  │
│  💡 Pushgateway still useful outside K8s (bare metal, VMs)       │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: The PCA exam covers modern Prometheus patterns. The sidecar pattern is the Kubernetes-native alternative to Pushgateway — know both and when to use each.

Observability Q29 of 209
What is "push proxying" and how does it differ from the Pushgateway?
A) Push proxying is the same as Pushgateway — the terms are interchangeable
B) Push proxying means Prometheus pushes metrics to Grafana instead of Grafana pulling from Prometheus
C) Push proxying means Prometheus pushes alerts to Alertmanager — it's the alerting push mechanism
D) Push proxying uses a proxy (like nginx or a Prometheus agent) that accepts pushed metrics and exposes them as a /metrics endpoint for Prometheus to scrape. Unlike Pushgateway, it acts as a transient buffer — metrics are forwarded and not persisted indefinitely, avoiding stale data
✅ Correct Answer: D

Push proxying is a hybrid pattern: apps push metrics to a local proxy/agent (e.g., Prometheus agent mode, Telegraf, or a custom receiver), which buffers them and exposes a /metrics endpoint. Prometheus scrapes the proxy using the standard Pull mechanism. This gives apps the convenience of Push while maintaining Prometheus's Pull architecture. Unlike the Pushgateway, push proxies typically don't persist metrics long-term — they act as transient buffers, so stale data isn't an issue. The Prometheus agent mode (introduced in v2.32) is purpose-built for this: it receives remote writes and exposes them for scraping.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Push Proxy vs Pushgateway — Key Differences
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  PUSHGATEWAY                                                      │
│    App → pushes metrics → Pushgateway (stores FOREVER)           │
│    Prometheus scrapes Pushgateway                                 │
│    ❌ Metrics persist until manually deleted                     │
│    ❌ Single instance (SPOF)                                     │
│                                                                  │
│  PUSH PROXY (Prometheus Agent mode)                               │
│    Apps → push (remote write) → Agent (buffers, exposes /metrics)│
│    Prometheus scrapes Agent                                       │
│    ✅ Metrics are transient (window-based)                       │
│    ✅ Can run multiple agents (no SPOF)                          │
│    ✅ Standard Prometheus scrape semantics                       │
│                                                                  │
│  USE CASES:                                                       │
│    Pushgateway:  legacy/external batch jobs, simple setups       │
│    Push proxy:   modern microservices, K8s environments,         │
│                   when you want Push convenience + Pull benefits │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 Prometheus agent mode (--enable-feature=agent) = official    │
│     push proxy — forwards to remote write AND exposes /metrics   │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: The PCA exam expects you to understand the Pushgateway and its alternatives. Push proxying via Prometheus agent mode is the modern approach for environments that prefer push semantics.

1.5 Service DiscoveryQ30-Q36

Observability Q30 of 209
Why is service discovery essential for Prometheus in dynamic environments like Kubernetes, and what problem does it solve compared to static target lists?
A) Service discovery is optional — you can manually list all IPs in static_configs and it works fine at any scale
B) In dynamic environments, pods are created/destroyed constantly with new IPs. Service discovery (kubernetes_sd_configs) automatically finds targets based on labels/annotations without manual IP updates. Without it, you'd be constantly editing prometheus.yml every time a pod scales or restarts — impossible at scale
C) Service discovery only matters for DNS — it resolves service names to IPs once at startup
D) Service discovery replaces the need for /metrics endpoints — targets are discovered and metrics are pushed automatically
✅ Correct Answer: B

In Kubernetes, pod IPs are ephemeral — every restart gets a new IP, every scale-up creates new pods with new IPs. Service discovery (kubernetes_sd_configs) watches the Kubernetes API for pods/services/endpoints matching your criteria and automatically updates the target list in Prometheus. Instead of maintaining a static list of 50 pod IPs (that change daily), you write a rule: "scrape all pods with label app=anihpj-api in namespace anihpj." Prometheus keeps the target list in sync automatically. For anihpj, as pods scale from 3→10 replicas, Prometheus automatically discovers and scrapes all 10.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Service Discovery — Automatic Target Updates vs Manual static_configs
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  WITHOUT service discovery (static_configs):                      │
│    prometheus.yml:                                                │
│    - targets: ['10.244.1.5:8000', '10.244.1.6:8000']            │
│    ❌ Pod restarts → new IP → update prometheus.yml manually     │
│    ❌ Scale 3→10 → add 7 new IPs → reload Prometheus              │
│    ❌ Doesn't scale beyond ~20 targets                            │
│                                                                  │
│  WITH kubernetes_sd_configs:                                      │
│    prometheus.yml:                                                │
│    - job_name: 'anihpj-api'                                       │
│      kubernetes_sd_configs:                                       │
│      - role: pod                                                  │
│        namespaces: [anihpj]                                       │
│      relabel_configs:                                             │
│      - source_labels: [__meta_kubernetes_pod_label_app]          │
│        regex: anihpj-api                                          │
│        action: keep                                               │
│                                                                  │
│    ✅ Pod restarts → new IP → discovered automatically            │
│    ✅ Scale 3→10 → Prometheus sees 10 targets within seconds     │
│    ✅ Scale 10→3 → stale targets removed automatically            │
│    ✅ Works for 1000s of targets                                  │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 IS is the bridge between dynamic infrastructure and static    │
│     scrape configs. Without it, Prometheus can't work in K8s.    │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Service discovery is a core Prometheus concept. You must understand that kubernetes_sd_configs is how Prometheus survives in dynamic environments — without it, you're stuck with manual IP management that doesn't scale.

Observability Q31 of 209
What are the main service discovery mechanisms Prometheus supports, and when would you use each?
A) static_configs (hardcoded IPs, small/static infra), file_sd_configs (JSON/YAML files from CMDB/scripts), kubernetes_sd_configs (automatic K8s pod/service/endpoint discovery), dns_sd_configs (DNS SRV/A records), ec2_sd_configs (AWS EC2 instances), consul_sd_configs (HashiCorp Consul) — choose based on where your targets live
B) Only kubernetes_sd_configs — all other methods are deprecated
C) Only static_configs and file_sd_configs — DNS and K8s SD are too unreliable
D) Only file_sd_configs — you should always generate target files from a script
✅ Correct Answer: A

Prometheus supports multiple service discovery mechanisms, each suited for different environments: static_configs — manual target lists for small/static setups. file_sd_configs — JSON/YAML files (can be generated by scripts/CMDB). kubernetes_sd_configs — the go-to for K8s, discovers pods/services/endpoints/nodes/ingresses. dns_sd_configs — DNS SRV records (good for Consul DNS interface). ec2_sd_configs — AWS EC2 instances with tag filtering (good for anihpj if running on EC2). consul_sd_configs — native Consul integration. For anihpj on Kubernetes, we use kubernetes_sd_configs; for any external services (RDS, ElastiCache), we use static_configs or dns_sd_configs.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Service Discovery Methods — When to Use Each
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  SD METHOD              BEST FOR                anihpj USE CASE  │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  static_configs         Fixed IPs, small infra  External RDS DB  │
│  file_sd_configs        Script-generated lists  CMDB integration │
│  kubernetes_sd_configs  ★ K8s workloads          anihpj pods      │
│  dns_sd_configs         DNS-based discovery      Consul services │
│  ec2_sd_configs         AWS EC2 instances        EC2-based nodes │
│  consul_sd_configs      Consul service mesh      Multi-DC setups │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  💡 You can COMBINE multiple SD methods in one Prometheus config │
│  💡 Each SD method produces __meta_* labels for relabeling       │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: You must know the different SD methods and their use cases. The exam will likely focus on kubernetes_sd_configs (for K8s environments) and static_configs (for everything else).

Observability Q32 of 209
You need to discover ALL anihpj pods in the "anihpj" namespace that have the annotation prometheus.io/scrape: "true". Which kubernetes_sd_configs role and relabel_config should you use?
A) role: service — services automatically discover pods. No relabeling needed
B) role: node — nodes discover pods running on them. Filter with namespace label
C) role: pod — discovers pod IPs directly. relabel_configs with action: keep on __meta_kubernetes_pod_annotation_prometheus_io_scrape matching "true", and __meta_kubernetes_namespace matching "anihpj"
D) role: endpoints — endpoints are always in sync with pods. No annotation filtering needed
✅ Correct Answer: C

Use role: pod with kubernetes_sd_configs to discover pod IPs directly. Apply relabel_configs to filter by annotation and namespace: action: keep with source_labels: [__meta_kubernetes_pod_annotation_prometheus_io_scrape] and regex: true. This is the standard Prometheus-on-Kubernetes pattern: pods opt-in to scraping via the prometheus.io/scrape annotation. The pod role discovers each pod's IP directly so Prometheus scrapes http://<pod-ip>:<port>/metrics.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kubernetes SD — pod Role with Annotation Filtering
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  prometheus.yml scrape_config for anihpj pods:                    │
│                                                                  │
│  - job_name: 'anihpj-pods'                                        │
│    kubernetes_sd_configs:                                         │
│    - role: pod                                                    │
│      namespaces:                                                  │
│        names: [anihpj]                                            │
│    relabel_configs:                                               │
│    - source_labels:                                               │
│        [__meta_kubernetes_pod_annotation_prometheus_io_scrape]   │
│      action: keep                                                 │
│      regex: true                                                  │
│    - source_labels: [__meta_kubernetes_pod_annotation_prometheus_│
│        io_path]                                                   │
│      action: replace                                              │
│      target_label: __metrics_path__                               │
│      regex: (.+)                                                  │
│    - source_labels: [__address__, __meta_kubernetes_pod_annotatio│
│        n_prometheus_io_port]                                      │
│      action: replace                                              │
│      target_label: __address__                                    │
│      regex: ([^:]+)(?::\d+)?;(\d+)                               │
│      replacement: $1:$2                                           │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 pod role = discover each pod's IP                             │
│  💡 annotation prometheus.io/scrape: "true" = opt-in to scraping  │
│  💡 This is the STANDARD pattern for Prometheus on Kubernetes     │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: The PCA exam expects you to understand kubernetes_sd_configs roles (pod, service, endpoints, node, ingress) and how relabel_configs filter discovered targets. This is a bread-and-butter PCA topic.

What does dns_sd_configs do and when would you use it for anihpj service discovery?
A) dns_sd_configs replaces CoreDNS in Kubernetes — it's an alternative DNS server
B) dns_sd_configs is deprecated — use kubernetes_sd_configs for all DNS-related discovery
C) dns_sd_configs monitors DNS server performance — it's for DNS observability, not service discovery

Observability Q35 of 209
How does Prometheus discover anihpj pods using kubernetes_sd_configs with the pod role and label selectors?
A) kubernetes_sd_configs with role: pod queries the K8s API for all pods. relabel_configs with action: keep filters by label (__meta_kubernetes_pod_label_app = anihpj-api). The remaining pods' IPs become scrape targets. Prometheus scrapes http://<pod-ip>:<port>/metrics directly
B) kubernetes_sd_configs scrapes the Kubernetes dashboard for pod lists — it doesn't query the API
C) Pods register themselves with Prometheus via a sidecar — SD is not involved in pod discovery
D) kubernetes_sd_configs only works with Services, not pods — you must create a Service for every pod
✅ Correct Answer: A

The pod role in kubernetes_sd_configs queries the Kubernetes API (/api/v1/pods) to discover all pods. Each pod gets a set of __meta_kubernetes_* labels (pod name, namespace, labels, annotations, node name, IP). relabel_configs then filters: keep only pods where __meta_kubernetes_pod_label_app matches anihpj-api, and __meta_kubernetes_namespace matches anihpj. The surviving pods' IPs (from __address__ = pod_ip:port) become scrape targets. This is how anihpj pods are discovered without manual configuration.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Pod Discovery Flow — K8s API → Relabeling → Scrape Targets
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Step 1: Query K8s API                                            │
│    GET /api/v1/pods?namespace=anihpj                              │
│    → Returns ALL pods in namespace                                │
│                                                                  │
│  Step 2: Each pod gets __meta labels                              │
│    pod: anihpj-api-7d8f9-abcde                                    │
│    __meta_kubernetes_pod_ip: 10.244.1.23                          │
│    __meta_kubernetes_pod_label_app: anihpj-api                    │
│    __meta_kubernetes_pod_label_version: v2.3.1                    │
│    __meta_kubernetes_namespace: anihpj                            │
│    __meta_kubernetes_pod_annotation_prometheus_io_scrape: true    │
│    __meta_kubernetes_pod_container_port_number: 8000              │
│                                                                  │
│  Step 3: relabel_configs filters                                  │
│    Keep: app=anihpj-api → ✅                                      │
│    Drop: app=anihpj-redis → ❌ (not an API pod)                   │
│    Drop: app=nginx-ingress → ❌ (not in anihpj namespace)         │
│                                                                  │
│  Step 4: Final scrape target                                      │
│    http://10.244.1.23:8000/metrics                                │
│    labels: {job="anihpj-api", namespace="anihpj",                 │
│             pod="anihpj-api-7d8f9-abcde"}                         │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 3 anihpj-api pods → 3 targets → all scraped automatically    │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: This is the core of Prometheus-on-Kubernetes. You must understand the flow: K8s API → __meta labels → relabel_configs filtering → scrape targets. The exam WILL test this.
