1.6 SLOs, SLAs & SLIsQ37-Q43

Observability Q37 of 209
Define SLI, SLO, and SLA. What is the relationship between these three concepts in site reliability engineering?
A) They are three interchangeable terms for the same concept — service availability
B) SLI = Service License Indicator, SLO = Service License Objective, SLA = Service License Agreement — they're legal terms for software licensing
C) SLI = how much you charge (pricing), SLO = how many customers you have (target), SLA = customer support response time
D) SLI (Service Level Indicator) = what you measure (p99 latency = 450ms). SLO (Service Level Objective) = your internal target (99.9% of requests < 500ms). SLA (Service Level Agreement) = your external promise with consequences (99.5% availability or refund). SLI → SLO → SLA: measure → target → contract
✅ Correct Answer: D

SLI (Service Level Indicator) is the actual measurement: "p99 latency = 450ms over the last 30 days." SLO (Service Level Objective) is your internal target: "99.9% of requests must complete within 500ms." SLA (Service Level Agreement) is the external contract: "If availability drops below 99.5%, customers get 25% refund." The relationship: SLI tells you if you're meeting your SLO. SLO should be tighter than SLA (e.g., SLO = 99.9%, SLA = 99.5%) so you fix problems before customers notice. For anihpj/jobpost: SLI = measured availability from Prometheus, SLO = 99.9% monthly uptime, SLA = 99.5% or credit to employers.

📝 Detailed Explanation
SLI → SLO → SLA — The Reliability Stack
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  SLA (Service Level Agreement) — External Promise                │
│  "99.5% uptime or 25% refund"                                    │
│  ├─ Legal/financial consequences if violated                     │
│  ├─ Set conservatively (easier to meet than SLO)                 │
│  └─ Example: anihpj SLA = 99.5% monthly uptime                   │
│                                                                  │
│  SLO (Service Level Objective) — Internal Target                 │
│  "99.9% of requests < 500ms p99 latency"                         │
│  ├─ Tighter than SLA (catches problems earlier)                  │
│  ├─ Drives error budget decisions (pause features if violated)   │
│  └─ Example: anihpj SLO = 99.9% availability                    │
│                                                                  │
│  SLI (Service Level Indicator) — What You Measure                │
│  "p99 latency = 450ms (meets SLO of 500ms)"                      │
│  ├─ Actual measurement from Prometheus metrics                   │
│  ├─ Calculated continuously: SLI = good_requests / total_requests│
│  └─ Example: anihpj SLI = 99.95% availability this month         │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 SLO should ALWAYS be tighter than SLA (safety margin)        │
│  💡 If SLI < SLO → burning error budget → take action            │
│  💡 If SLI < SLA → legal/financial consequences                  │
│  💡 Prometheus calculates SLIs; humans set SLOs/SLAs             │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: SLI/SLO/SLA are fundamental SRE concepts tested on the PCA. You must know the hierarchy (SLI=measurement, SLO=target, SLA=contract) and that Prometheus provides the SLIs that power SLO monitoring.

Observability Q38 of 209
What is an "error budget" and how does it relate to SLOs?
A) Error budget = 1 - SLO. If SLO is 99.9% availability, error budget = 0.1% downtime (~43 minutes/month). When error budget is consumed, you stop deploying features and focus on reliability. It quantifies "how much failure is acceptable"
B) Error budget is the money allocated for fixing bugs — it's a financial term unrelated to SLOs
C) Error budget = number of bugs allowed per sprint. SLO tracks bugs; error budget limits them
D) Error budget is the same as SLO — the terms are synonymous
✅ Correct Answer: A

The error budget is the amount of "acceptable failure" derived from your SLO: Error Budget = 1 - SLO. If anihpj SLO = 99.9% availability, the error budget = 0.1% = ~43 minutes of downtime per month. Engineering teams "spend" this budget on: (1) inevitable failures (bugs, outages), (2) planned maintenance, (3) risky deployments. When the budget is exhausted (too much downtime), the policy is: STOP deploying features, focus ALL effort on reliability improvements until the budget recovers. This balances innovation (features) with stability (reliability).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Error Budget — The Innovation vs Reliability Balance
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  anihpj SLO: 99.9% uptime per month                               │
│  Error Budget: 0.1% = ~43 minutes/month                          │
│                                                                  │
│  MONTHLY ERROR BUDGET TRACKER:                                    │
│  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐         │
│  │ ██████████████████████████████████████████████████ │ 43 min  │
│  │ ████████████████████░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░ │ 20 min  │
│  │ used: 23 min remaining                             │ used    │
│  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘         │
│                                                                  │
│  BUDGET REMAINING > 50%:  ✅ Deploy features freely              │
│  BUDGET REMAINING < 50%:  ⚠️ Slow down, increase testing         │
│  BUDGET REMAINING < 10%:  🔴 FREEZE all deployments              │
│  BUDGET EXHAUSTED:        🚨 All hands on reliability            │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 Error budget = "permission to fail"                           │
│  💡 Without it: either never deploy (too scared) or deploy       │
│     recklessly (too careless)                                     │
│  💡 Prometheus monitors SLI → calculates error budget burn rate  │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Error budgets are a core SRE concept. The PCA exam tests your ability to calculate error budgets and understand their role in balancing reliability vs feature velocity.

Observability Q39 of 209
Using Prometheus, how would you define and measure SLIs for anihpj/jobpost — covering availability, latency, throughput, and correctness?
A) SLIs can't be measured with Prometheus — you need a dedicated APM tool like Datadog or New Relic
B) SLIs are manually calculated once per month by looking at Grafana dashboards — no automation needed
C) Availability: up{job="anihpj-api"}/count(up{job="anihpj-api"}). Latency: histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket[30d])). Throughput: rate(http_requests_total[30d]). Correctness: rate(http_requests_total{status="200"}[30d])/rate(http_requests_total[30d]). All computed continuously by Prometheus recording rules
D) SLIs are defined in the SLA contract — no measurement needed, just legal compliance
✅ Correct Answer: C

Prometheus is the primary tool for measuring SLIs. For anihpj: Availability: ratio of successful scrapes (up metric). Latency: p99 request duration from histogram — histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket[30d])). Throughput: requests per second — rate(http_requests_total[30d]). Correctness: ratio of successful (2xx) to total responses. These should be pre-computed via recording rules for efficiency and tracked on SLO dashboards with error budget burn-down charts.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj SLIs — Prometheus Recording Rules
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  # Recording rules for anihpj SLIs (rules.yml)                    │
│  groups:                                                          │
│  - name: anihpj_slis                                              │
│    interval: 30s                                                  │
│    rules:                                                         │
│                                                                  │
│    # AVAILABILITY: ratio of UP targets                            │
│    - record: sli:availability                                     │
│      expr: avg(up{job="anihpj-api"})                              │
│                                                                  │
│    # LATENCY: p99 request duration                                │
│    - record: sli:latency_p99                                      │
│      expr: histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(http_request_duration_   │
│             seconds_bucket{job="anihpj-api"}[30d]))               │
│                                                                  │
│    # THROUGHPUT: requests per second                              │
│    - record: sli:throughput                                       │
│      expr: rate(http_requests_total{job="anihpj-api"}[30d])       │
│                                                                  │
│    # ERROR RATE: failed / total                                   │
│    - record: sli:error_ratio                                      │
│      expr: rate(http_requests_total{job="anihpj-api",             │
│             status=~"5.."}[30d]) / rate(http_requests_total[30d]) │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 Recording rules pre-compute SLIs for fast dashboard queries  │
│  💡 SLO compliance = (SLI >= SLO target) → boolean               │
│  💡 Error budget burn rate = (1 - SLO) / (1 - SLI) → alert       │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: The PCA exam expects you to know how Prometheus calculates SLIs. You must understand recording rules for SLIs and how SLO compliance is derived from Prometheus metrics.

Observability Q40 of 209
What is burn rate alerting, and why is it better than simple threshold alerting for SLOs?
A) Burn rate alerting fires when CPU temperature exceeds 80°C — it's for hardware monitoring only
B) Burn rate = how fast you're consuming your error budget relative to the SLO period. A 1-hour burn rate of 14.4x means you burned 14.4 hours of error budget in 1 hour — alert before the full month's budget is gone. Better than threshold alerting because it catches FAST error budget consumption (rapid outage) AND SLOW consumption (gradual degradation)
C) Burn rate alerting is the same as threshold alerting — they're different names for "alert when metric > threshold"
D) Burn rate alerting means alerting when Prometheus disk usage is nearly full — it's a storage alert
✅ Correct Answer: B

Burn rate alerting measures how fast you're consuming your error budget. A 30-day error budget of 0.1% (43 min) consumed in 1 hour = burn rate of 14.4x — you'll exhaust the full month's budget in ~3 hours if this continues. Multi-window alerting catches both: short window (1h, burn rate > 14.4x → "CRITICAL: major outage!") and long window (6h, burn rate > 1x → "WARNING: error budget depleting faster than normal"). This is superior to simple threshold alerting ("alert if p99 > 500ms") because it accounts for the RATE of failure, not just the presence of failure.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Burn Rate Alerting — Multi-Window, Multi-Threshold
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  anihpj SLO: 99.9% availability → Error Budget = 0.1% (43 min)  │
│                                                                  │
│  ALERT WINDOWS:                                                   │
│                                                                  │
│  SHORT WINDOW (1 hour): detects RAPID outages                     │
│    Burn rate > 14.4x → CRITICAL: "You'll exhaust monthly budget   │
│    in ~3 hours at this rate. Major incident!"                     │
│                                                                  │
│  MEDIUM WINDOW (6 hours): detects DEGRADATION                     │
│    Burn rate > 2x → WARNING: "Error rate is elevated. Budget      │
│    depleting faster than normal. Investigate."                    │
│                                                                  │
│  PromQL for 1-hour burn rate:                                     │
│    (1 - sli:availability) / (1 - SLO_target)                     │
│    → if > 14.4: CRITICAL alert                                    │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 Burn rate = "how many X the normal rate are we failing?"     │
│  💡 Multi-window = catch FAST problems AND SLOW problems          │
│  💡 Better than "p99 > 500ms" because it considers DURATION      │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Burn rate alerting is an advanced PCA topic. Know the difference between simple threshold alerting ("p99 > 500ms") and burn rate alerting ("consuming budget 14x faster than normal").

Observability Q41 of 209
For anihpj/jobpost, you set an SLO of 99.9% availability. How many minutes of downtime per month does this allow?
A) 0.1% of 43,200 minutes/month = ~43 minutes of allowed downtime per month
B) 99.9% means zero downtime — any outage violates the SLO
C) 0.1% of 720 hours = 0.72 hours = ~43 seconds of downtime
D) 99.9% of 30 days = 29.97 days uptime, 0.03 days = ~43 hours downtime
✅ Correct Answer: A

30 days × 24 hours × 60 minutes = 43,200 minutes/month. Error budget = 100% - 99.9% = 0.1%. 0.1% × 43,200 = 43.2 minutes/month of allowed downtime. Common SLO references: 99% = 432 min/month (7.2 hrs), 99.9% = 43 min/month, 99.99% = 4.3 min/month, 99.999% ("five nines") = 26 seconds/month. For anihpj, 43 minutes/month is reasonable — covers occasional deployment issues, database failovers, and cloud provider hiccups without being overly restrictive.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Availability SLOs — Downtime Allowed
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  SLO          Error Budget    Downtime/Month   Downtime/Year     │
│  ───────────  ──────────────  ───────────────  ────────────────  │
│  99%          1%              432 min (7.2h)   3.65 days         │
│  99.5%        0.5%            216 min (3.6h)   1.83 days         │
│  99.9%   ←    0.1%            43.2 min         8.76 hours        │
│  99.95%       0.05%           21.6 min         4.38 hours        │
│  99.99%       0.01%           4.32 min         52.6 minutes      │
│  99.999%      0.001%          25.9 seconds     5.26 minutes      │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 Each "nine" costs ~10× more to achieve                       │
│  💡 Most web apps target 99.9% or 99.95%                         │
│  💡 anihpj/jobpost: 99.9% = reasonable balance of cost/reliability│
│  💡 PCA tip: memorize the conversion — 30 days = 43,200 minutes  │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: SLO calculations are tested on the PCA. Know that 30 days = 43,200 minutes and how to calculate error budgets at different SLO levels.

Observability Q42 of 209
Should your SLO be tighter than your SLA? Why or why not?
A) No — SLA should be tighter because it has financial penalties and must be stricter
B) Yes — SLO (internal target) should always be tighter than SLA (external promise). Example: SLO = 99.9% (43 min downtime), SLA = 99.5% (216 min downtime). This creates a 173-minute "safety buffer" — your SLO alerts fire and you fix problems BEFORE customers notice and BEFORE SLA violations trigger refunds
C) SLO and SLA should always be equal — having different numbers creates confusion
D) SLO is for latency, SLA is for availability — they measure different things so comparison is meaningless
✅ Correct Answer: B

The golden rule: SLO > SLA (SLO is tighter/more demanding). For anihpj, SLO = 99.9% (43 min), SLA = 99.5% (216 min). This 173-minute buffer means: your internal monitoring (Prometheus SLO alerts) detects issues at 43 minutes → team responds → fixes before 216 minutes → customers never notice → no SLA violation → no refunds. If SLO = SLA, you discover issues at the same time customers do (or worse, after they complain). The buffer is your early warning system.

📝 Detailed Explanation
SLO vs SLA — The Safety Buffer
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  anihpj/jobpost Reliability Targets:                               │
│                                                                  │
│  ┌─ SLA (external promise) ──────────────────────────────────┐  │
│  │  99.5% uptime = 216 min allowed downtime/month             │  │
│  │  "If we're down > 216 min → customers get refund"          │  │
│  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
│                    ↑↑↑ 173-MINUTE SAFETY BUFFER ↑↑↑               │
│  ┌─ SLO (internal target) ───────────────────────────────────┐  │
│  │  99.9% uptime = 43 min allowed downtime/month              │  │
│  │  "If we're down > 43 min → alert fires → team responds"    │  │
│  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
│                                                                  │
│  Scenario: Outage at 10:00 AM                                     │
│    10:43 AM → SLO Alert fires (team paged)                       │
│    11:15 AM → Team identifies and fixes issue                    │
│    Total downtime: 75 min                                         │
│    ❌ SLO violated (75 > 43) → postmortem, fix root cause        │
│    ✅ SLA NOT violated (75 < 216) → no customer impact, no refund │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 SLO buffer = time to detect + respond + fix before SLA breach│
│  💡 Without buffer: your first alert IS the customer complaint   │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: The PCA exam tests the relationship between SLOs and SLAs. Know: SLO > SLA (SLO tighter), SLO is your early warning, SLA is what customers see.

Observability Q43 of 209
Which statement best describes how Prometheus fits into an SLO-based monitoring strategy?
A) Prometheus only collects metrics — SLOs are managed by a separate legal/compliance system
B) SLOs are defined in Prometheus alerting rules directly — Prometheus IS the SLO manager
C) Prometheus has built-in SLO tracking via a dedicated SLO dashboard at /slo
D) Prometheus provides the raw metrics (SLIs) — recording rules pre-compute SLIs, alerting rules fire on SLO violations and error budget burn rates, Grafana dashboards visualize SLO compliance. Prometheus is the measurement engine; humans define SLOs; Alertmanager notifies on violations
✅ Correct Answer: D

Prometheus is the measurement engine for SLOs, not the SLO manager. The workflow: (1) Humans define SLOs (e.g., "99.9% of requests < 500ms p99"). (2) Prometheus recording rules compute SLIs from raw metrics. (3) Alerting rules fire when SLIs approach SLO thresholds (error budget burn rate alerts). (4) Alertmanager routes alerts to on-call teams. (5) Grafana dashboards visualize SLO compliance and error budget remaining. Prometheus doesn't define SLOs — it measures them, alerts on them, and provides the data for SLO dashboards. Tools like Pyrra or Sloth can layer on top for dedicated SLO management.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Prometheus Role in SLO-Based Monitoring
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  SLO MONITORING STACK:                                            │
│                                                                  │
│  1. HUMANS DEFINE:                                                │
│     "anihpj API: 99.9% of requests < 500ms p99"                  │
│                                                                  │
│  2. PROMETHEUS MEASURES (recording rules):                        │
│     sli:latency_p99 = histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(...))        │
│                                                                  │
│  3. PROMETHEUS ALERTS (alerting rules):                           │
│     sli:latency_p99 > 0.5  →  "SLO at risk!"                     │
│     error_budget_burn_rate > 14.4 → "CRITICAL: rapid burn!"      │
│                                                                  │
│  4. ALERTMANAGER ROUTES:                                          │
│     CRITICAL → PagerDuty (wake up on-call)                        │
│     WARNING  → Slack #anihpj-alerts (investigate during work)     │
│                                                                  │
│  5. GRAFANA VISUALIZES:                                           │
│     Error budget gauge: 73% remaining (green)                     │
│     SLO compliance: 99.95% this month (meets 99.9%)               │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 Prometheus = measurement + alerting engine                    │
│  💡 SLOs = human-defined targets                                  │
│  💡 Pyrra/Sloth = dedicated SLO tools on top of Prometheus        │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: This is the capstone of the Observability Concepts domain. You must understand Prometheus's role in the SLO ecosystem: it measures SLIs and alerts on violations, but humans define the SLOs. The PCA exam tests your understanding of this relationship.

🟢 Understanding Prometheus — Architecture, Configuration & Data Model

This category is about how Prometheus works internally. Think of it like learning the anatomy of a car before driving it — you need to understand the engine (TSDB), the controls (configuration), the dashboard (HTTP API), and the limitations (when you need Thanos/Cortex). For anihpj/jobpost, this means understanding exactly how Prometheus scrapes Django metrics, stores them, and makes them queryable.

Q44-Q85 42 Questions 🟢 Beginner

2.1 System ArchitectureQ44-Q53

Fundamentals Q44 of 209
What are the core components of the Prometheus server, and what does each do?
A) Prometheus has two components: a scraper and a database. The scraper collects metrics and stores them in PostgreSQL
B) Prometheus is a single binary with no internal components — it's monolithic by design
C) Retrieval (scrapes targets on schedule), TSDB (stores time series in custom database), HTTP Server (serves PromQL queries, UI, and API). These run as a single binary but are logically separate subsystems
D) Scraper (collects), Ingester (processes), Compactor (compresses), Query (serves queries) — Prometheus uses a microservices architecture
✅ Correct Answer: C

Prometheus is a single Go binary with three logical subsystems: Retrieval (scrape engine — discovers targets via SD, scrapes /metrics on scrape_interval, handles TLS/auth), TSDB (Time Series Database — writes scraped metrics to WAL → head block → compacted persistent blocks, handles retention), and HTTP Server (serves PromQL queries via /api/v1/query, the expression browser at /graph, target status at /targets, and configuration at /config). All three run in one process — no microservices, no external dependencies (except Alertmanager for alerts).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Prometheus Architecture — Single Binary, Three Subsystems
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  PROMETHEUS SERVER (single binary — prometheus)                   │
│                                                                  │
│  ┌─────────────────┐                                             │
│  │   RETRIEVAL     │ ← scrapes targets on scrape_interval        │
│  │   (Scraper)     │    Uses service discovery (K8s SD, etc.)    │
│  │                 │    Handles TLS, auth, relabeling            │
│  └────────┬────────┘                                             │
│           │ metrics data                                          │
│           ↓                                                       │
│  ┌─────────────────┐                                             │
│  │     TSDB        │ ← stores time series                        │
│  │   (Storage)     │    WAL → Head Block → Persistent Blocks     │
│  │                 │    Compaction, retention, snapshots         │
│  └────────┬────────┘                                             │
│           │ stored data                                           │
│           ↓                                                       │
│  ┌─────────────────┐                                             │
│  │   HTTP SERVER   │ ← serves queries, UI, API                   │
│  │   (Query API)   │    /api/v1/query, /graph, /targets          │
│  │                 │    PromQL evaluation engine                 │
│  └────────┬────────┘                                             │
│           │                                                       │
│           ├──→ /api/v1/query (PromQL queries from Grafana)       │
│           ├──→ /graph (Expression browser for humans)            │
│           ├──→ /targets (Scrape target status)                   │
│           ├──→ /metrics (Prometheus's own metrics)               │
│           └──→ /-/reload (Hot-reload configuration)              │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 Single binary = no distributed components by default         │
│  💡 Alertmanager is SEPARATE (not part of Prometheus binary)     │
│  💡 For HA: run 2 identical Prometheus instances                 │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Understanding Prometheus architecture is fundamental. Know that it's a single binary with retrieval + TSDB + HTTP server, and that Alertmanager is a separate component. The PCA exam will test this architecture knowledge.

Fundamentals Q45 of 209
Describe the Prometheus scrape loop — from service discovery to stored time series. What happens at each step?
A) Scrape loop: 1. Query DNS, 2. Connect to targets, 3. Download all data, 4. Store in PostgreSQL
B) 1. Service Discovery (find targets via K8s SD, static configs, etc.), 2. Relabeling (filter/transform targets and labels), 3. Scrape (HTTP GET /metrics on each target), 4. Parse (text exposition format → samples), 5. Store (append to head block via WAL). This cycle runs every scrape_interval per job
C) 1. Targets push metrics, 2. Prometheus receives them, 3. Alerts are evaluated, 4. Metrics are forwarded to Grafana
D) The scrape loop only runs once at startup — Prometheus uses change data capture after initial load
✅ Correct Answer: B

The scrape loop is the heartbeat of Prometheus, running every scrape_interval (default 15s) per job: (1) Service Discovery — query configured SD (Kubernetes API, DNS, file) to get current target list. (2) Relabeling — apply relabel_configs to filter targets and modify labels. (3) Scrape — HTTP GET each target's /metrics endpoint. (4) Parse — parse the Prometheus exposition format text into time series samples. (5) Store — append samples to the head block via the Write-Ahead Log (WAL) for durability. This cycle runs continuously for every job independently — one job may scrape every 15s, another every 60s.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Scrape Loop — 5 Steps from Discovery to Storage
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  SCRAPE LOOP (runs every scrape_interval per job)                     │
│                                                                      │
│  ┌─────────────────┐                                                 │
│  │ 1. DISCOVERY     │  K8s API → get pods with app=anihpj-api        │
│  │   10.244.1.23,   │  static_configs → RDS endpoint                 │
│  │   10.244.1.24     │  → 3 targets found                             │
│  └────────┬────────┘                                                 │
│           ↓                                                          │
│  ┌─────────────────┐                                                 │
│  │ 2. RELABELING    │  Keep: namespace=anihpj, app=anihpj-api        │
│  │   Add labels:    │  Drop: kube-system pods                        │
│  │   job, namespace │  → 2 targets after filtering                   │
│  └────────┬────────┘                                                 │
│           ↓                                                          │
│  ┌─────────────────┐                                                 │
│  │ 3. SCRAPE        │  GET http://10.244.1.23:8000/metrics           │
│  │   HTTP GET        │  GET http://10.244.1.24:8000/metrics           │
│  │   /metrics       │  → text exposition format response             │
│  └────────┬────────┘                                                 │
│           ↓                                                          │
│  ┌─────────────────┐                                                 │
│  │ 4. PARSE         │  http_requests_total{status="200"} 54321       │
│  │   Text → Samples │  → {metric: http_requests_total,               │
│  │                  │     labels: {status:200}, value: 54321}        │
│  └────────┬────────┘                                                 │
│           ↓                                                          │
│  ┌─────────────────┐                                                 │
│  │ 5. STORE         │  Append to WAL (durability)                    │
│  │   WAL → Head     │  Append to Head Block (in-memory, queryable)   │
│  │   Block          │  → Time series ready for PromQL queries        │
│  └─────────────────┘                                                 │
│                                                                      │
│  💡 Steps 1-2 happen ONCE per scrape interval per job                │
│  💡 Steps 3-5 happen for EACH target in the job                      │
│  💡 Scrape timeout < scrape_interval (default 10s < 15s)            │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: The PCA exam tests your understanding of the full scrape pipeline. Know the 5 steps and what happens at each stage — especially discovery, relabeling, and the parse-to-store flow.

Fundamentals Q46 of 209
Prometheus is a single-binary architecture with no built-in clustering. How do you achieve high availability with Prometheus?
A) Run 2+ identical Prometheus instances scraping the same targets. Each is independent — no coordination, no shared state. Use Thanos Query (or Grafana) to deduplicate data from both instances. This is the standard Prometheus HA pattern — simple, reliable, no clustering needed
B) Enable Prometheus cluster mode via --cluster.enable flag — Prometheus automatically forms a RAFT cluster
C) Use Prometheus Operator to create a StatefulSet with 3 replicas — they automatically replicate data
D) Prometheus cannot be highly available — it's designed for single-node deployment only
✅ Correct Answer: A

Prometheus achieves HA through redundancy, not clustering. Run 2 (or more) identical Prometheus instances — same config, same scrape targets, same rules. Each scrapes independently (slightly different timestamps → slightly different data). Thanos Query (or Grafana with multiple Prometheus datasources) deduplicates data from both instances. If one Prometheus dies, the other continues scraping — no data gap. Benefits: simple (no distributed consensus), each instance is independent (failure isolation), and the dedup layer handles the slight data differences from independent scraping.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Prometheus HA — Redundancy, Not Clustering
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  PROMETHEUS HA PATTERN:                                           │
│                                                                  │
│  ┌──────────────┐  scrape  ┌──────────────────┐                 │
│  │ Prometheus A │─────────→│ anihpj-api pods   │                 │
│  │ (instance 1) │          │ anihpj-db pod     │                 │
│  └──────┬───────┘          │ node_exporter     │                 │
│         │                  └──────────────────┘                 │
│         │                  ┌──────────────────┐                 │
│  ┌──────┴───────┐  scrape │ anihpj-api pods   │                 │
│  │ Prometheus B │─────────→│ anihpj-db pod     │                 │
│  │ (instance 2) │          │ node_exporter     │                 │
│  └──────┬───────┘          └──────────────────┘                 │
│         │                                                       │
│         ├────→ Thanos Query (deduplicates A + B)                │
│         │       → Grafana queries Thanos, sees deduped data     │
│         │                                                       │
│  💡 Both Prom instances scrape the SAME targets                  │
│  💡 Each is completely independent (different timestamps)       │
│  💡 Thanos Query deduplicates using replica labels              │
│  💡 No clustering, no shared storage, no consensus              │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Prometheus HA is a commonly tested concept. Know that HA = running identical instances (no clustering) + Thanos/Grafana deduplication.

Fundamentals Q47 of 209
What is Prometheus federation, and how does hierarchical federation work for multi-cluster monitoring?
A) Federation means all Prometheus instances share a single database — they write to the same TSDB
B) Federation means Prometheus instances form a Kubernetes cluster — each instance is a node in the cluster
C) Federation means Prometheus replicates data between instances in real-time using RAFT consensus
D) Federation: a global Prometheus scrapes the /federate endpoint of regional Prometheus instances, collecting aggregated metrics (not raw data). Hierarchical: global scrapes regional → regional scrapes local. Use match[] to select which metrics to pull up. Good for aggregating summary metrics across clusters without duplicating all raw data
✅ Correct Answer: D

Federation is Prometheus's built-in mechanism for hierarchical monitoring. A "global" Prometheus scrapes the /federate endpoint of regional Prometheus instances. The key: it pulls aggregated metrics (not raw data). For anihpj: 3 regional Prometheus instances (us-east, us-west, eu-west) each scrape local pods. A global Prometheus federates from all 3 — it might pull sum(rate(http_requests_total[5m])) by (region) from each to get global request rates, without pulling every individual pod's raw metrics. This saves bandwidth, storage, and query load on regional instances. Use match[] parameters to select which metrics to federate.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Prometheus Federation — Hierarchical Monitoring
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  HIERARCHICAL FEDERATION:                                         │
│                                                                  │
│  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
│  │              GLOBAL PROMETHEUS (aggregates)               │   │
│  │  scrape /federate from each regional instance             │   │
│  │  match[]: {job="anihpj-api"}  ← only pull API metrics    │   │
│  └────┬──────────────┬──────────────┬───────────────────────┘   │
│       │              │              │                             │
│       ↓              ↓              ↓                             │
│  ┌─────────┐   ┌─────────┐   ┌─────────┐                        │
│  │Regional │   │Regional │   │Regional │                        │
│  │us-east  │   │us-west  │   │eu-west  │                        │
│  │scrapes: │   │scrapes: │   │scrapes: │                        │
│  │50 pods  │   │50 pods  │   │50 pods  │                        │
│  └─────────┘   └─────────┘   └─────────┘                        │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 Global scrapes AGGREGATED metrics from regional instances    │
│  💡 match[] parameter = select which metrics to pull up          │
│  💡 Regional instances keep raw data; global sees summaries     │
│  💡 Federation vs Thanos: federation = built-in, simple;         │
│     Thanos = more powerful (global query, long-term storage)     │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Federation is a Prometheus-native feature for hierarchical monitoring. Know that it scrapes /federate (not /metrics), uses match[] to select metrics, and pulls aggregated data (not raw).

Fundamentals Q48 of 209
What problem does Thanos solve that Prometheus alone cannot, and what components does it add?
A) Thanos replaces Prometheus entirely — it's a complete monitoring system with its own TSDB
B) Thanos adds: global query view (Query), long-term storage in S3 (Sidecar + Store), downsampling (Compactor), and HA deduplication. Prometheus alone is limited to single-instance queries with local retention; Thanos extends it to global, scalable, long-term monitoring
C) Thanos is a Grafana plugin that adds alerting to Prometheus dashboards
D) Thanos is a replacement for Alertmanager — it handles alert routing at global scale
✅ Correct Answer: B

Thanos extends Prometheus with: Sidecar (uploads TSDB blocks to S3), Store Gateway (queries historical data from S3), Compactor (downsamples and compacts blocks in S3), Query (deduplicates and merges data from multiple Prometheus instances + Store Gateways), and Ruler (evaluates recording/alerting rules against Thanos Query). Together they give Prometheus: global query view across all instances, years of retention via cheap S3 storage, downsampling (5m → 1h resolution for old data), and HA deduplication. For anihpj: Prometheus retains 30 days locally, Thanos retains years in S3.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Thanos Architecture — Extending Prometheus
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  THANOS COMPONENTS:                                               │
│                                                                  │
│  ┌─────────┐   Sidecar    ┌─────────┐                            │
│  │Prometheus│─────────────→│   S3    │ ← Cheap, infinite storage │
│  │ (30d)   │  uploads     │ (years) │                            │
│  └─────────┘  blocks      └────┬────┘                            │
│                                │                                  │
│  ┌──────────────┐              │                                  │
│  │Store Gateway │←─────────────┘  queries historical blocks      │
│  └──────┬───────┘                                                 │
│         │                                                         │
│  ┌──────┴───────┐                                                 │
│  │Thanos Query  │ ← deduplicates Prom A + Prom B + S3 data       │
│  │(global view) │    Grafana connects HERE, not to Prometheus     │
│  └──────────────┘                                                 │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 Thanos = Prometheus on steroids (global, long-term, HA)      │
│  💡 PCA exam: know Thanos extends Prometheus, doesn't replace it │
│  💡 Alternative: Cortex / Grafana Mimir (similar goals)          │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: The PCA exam covers the Prometheus ecosystem. Know that Thanos extends Prometheus (global query, S3 storage, dedup) but doesn't replace it — Prometheus is still the scraper and local TSDB.

Fundamentals Q49 of 209
What is the Prometheus Operator, and what Kubernetes custom resources does it introduce?
A) Prometheus Operator is a kubectl plugin that simplifies running Prometheus commands — it doesn't create custom resources
B) It's a Grafana feature that connects Prometheus as a datasource — no Kubernetes resources involved
C) It's a Kubernetes operator that manages Prometheus stacks using CRDs: Prometheus (defines the Prometheus instance), ServiceMonitor (selects Services to scrape), PodMonitor (selects Pods to scrape), PrometheusRule (recording/alerting rules), AlertmanagerConfig (alert routing). It automates config generation via these CRDs instead of raw prometheus.yml
D) It's a Docker image that runs Prometheus — it's just a containerized version, nothing Kubernetes-specific
✅ Correct Answer: C

The Prometheus Operator (part of kube-prometheus-stack) is the standard way to run Prometheus on Kubernetes. Instead of manually writing prometheus.yml ConfigMaps and updating them when services change, you define CRDs: ServiceMonitor ("scrape Services with label app=anihpj-api on port 8000"), PodMonitor ("scrape Pods directly"), PrometheusRule (recording/alerting rules as Kubernetes resources). The Operator watches these CRDs and automatically generates and updates the Prometheus configuration — no manual prometheus.yml editing needed. This is how anihpj's Prometheus stack is managed on K8s.

What is the relationship between Prometheus and Grafana, and why are they almost always deployed together?
A) Grafana is part of Prometheus — it's the built-in dashboarding component. Installing Prometheus automatically installs Grafana
B) Grafana replaces Prometheus's HTTP API — all PromQL queries go through Grafana first
C) Prometheus and Grafana are competitors — you choose one or the other for monitoring
D) They are separate, independent projects. Prometheus handles metrics collection + storage + PromQL querying. Grafana handles visualization + dashboards + alerting UI. They're deployed together because Prometheus has no built-in dashboarding (its /graph is primitive), and Grafana provides rich, customizable dashboards that query Prometheus (and Loki, Tempo, etc.) as datasources
✅ Correct Answer: D

Prometheus and Grafana are separate CNCF projects with a symbiotic relationship. Prometheus does metrics (collect, store, query via PromQL). Grafana does visualization (dashboards, panels, alerts UI). Prometheus's built-in /graph is a basic expression browser — not a dashboarding tool. Grafana provides rich dashboards with template variables, annotations, alerting UI, and the ability to query multiple datasources simultaneously (Prometheus for metrics + Loki for logs + Tempo for traces). They're almost always deployed together because each does what the other doesn't. For anihpj: Prometheus scrapes and stores metrics; Grafana displays the RED/USE dashboards.

Fundamentals Q51 of 209
What is the Alertmanager, and how does it relate to Prometheus alerting rules?
A) Alertmanager is a built-in component of Prometheus that handles alert routing — no separate installation needed
B) Alertmanager is a SEPARATE service that receives alerts fired by Prometheus alerting rules. Prometheus evaluates alerting rules and sends firing alerts to Alertmanager, which handles deduplication, grouping, inhibition, silencing, and routing to receivers (email, Slack, PagerDuty). They are two distinct binaries
C) Alertmanager replaces Prometheus alerting rules — you write alerts directly in Alertmanager, not in Prometheus
D) Alertmanager is a Grafana plugin that adds alert visualization to dashboards
✅ Correct Answer: B

Alertmanager is a separate, independent binary from Prometheus. The workflow: (1) Prometheus evaluates alerting rules every evaluation_interval. (2) When a rule condition is true for the for duration, Prometheus fires the alert. (3) Prometheus sends firing alerts to Alertmanager's HTTP API. (4) Alertmanager handles: deduplication (same alert from multiple Prometheus instances), grouping (combine similar alerts into one notification), inhibition (suppress low-priority if high-priority is firing), silencing (mute alerts during maintenance), and routing (send to Slack, PagerDuty, email based on labels). For anihpj: Prometheus fires "API error rate > 5%", Alertmanager routes to #anihpj-alerts Slack and pages on-call via PagerDuty.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Prometheus + Alertmanager — Alert Lifecycle
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  ALERT LIFECYCLE:                                                     │
│                                                                      │
│  ┌─────────────┐    fires alert     ┌──────────────┐                 │
│  │ Prometheus  │──────────────────→ │ Alertmanager │                 │
│  │ (evaluates  │  POST /api/v1/     │ (processes)  │                 │
│  │  rules)     │  alerts            │              │                 │
│  └─────────────┘                    └──────┬───────┘                 │
│                                           │                          │
│                    ┌──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┐   │
│                    │                      │                      │   │
│                    ↓                      ↓                      ↓   │
│              ┌──────────┐          ┌──────────┐          ┌──────────┐│
│              │  Slack   │          │PagerDuty │          │  Email   ││
│              │#anihpj-  │          │  wake up │          │  daily   ││
│              │ alerts   │          │  on-call │          │  digest  ││
│              └──────────┘          └──────────┘          └──────────┘│
│                                                                      │
│  Alertmanager features:                                               │
│    🔄 Deduplication: same alert from Prom A + Prom B → 1 notification│
│    📦 Grouping: 50 similar alerts → 1 grouped notification            │
│    🔇 Inhibition: "DB is down" firing → suppress "API errors"         │
│    🤫 Silencing: maintenance window → mute all anihpj alerts          │
│    🚦 Routing: severity=critical → PagerDuty; severity=warning→Slack │
│                                                                      │
│  💡 Prometheus = evaluates rules + fires alerts                      │
│  💡 Alertmanager = processes + routes + manages alerts               │
│  💡 They are SEPARATE binaries, deployed together                    │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: You must know that Alertmanager is a separate service from Prometheus. Prometheus evaluates alerting RULES, Alertmanager handles alert ROUTING. The PCA exam tests this distinction.

Fundamentals Q52 of 209
What is remote write and remote read in Prometheus, and when would you use each?
A) Remote write sends alerts to Alertmanager; remote read fetches rules from a remote server
B) Remote write backs up prometheus.yml to a remote Git repo; remote read restores configuration from Git
C) Remote write sends PromQL queries to remote Prometheus instances; remote read receives query results
D) Remote write forwards scraped metrics to an external storage system (Thanos, Cortex, Mimir, VictoriaMetrics) for long-term retention. Remote read allows Prometheus to query external TSDBs as if they were local data. Both use Snappy-compressed protobufs over HTTP
✅ Correct Answer: D

Remote write forwards every scraped sample (in real-time) to an external compatible storage system — Thanos Receive, Cortex, Grafana Mimir, VictoriaMetrics, or cloud services (Grafana Cloud, AWS AMP). This gives you long-term retention beyond Prometheus's local TSDB. Remote read allows Prometheus to query external TSDBs transparently — when a PromQL query needs data outside local retention, Prometheus can fetch it from the remote storage. For anihpj: remote write to Thanos Receive → S3 for years of retention, while local Prometheus keeps 30 days for fast queries.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Remote Write/Read — Extending Prometheus Storage
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  REMOTE WRITE (Prometheus → External Storage):                        │
│                                                                      │
│  ┌─────────────┐   remote_write    ┌──────────────────┐             │
│  │ Prometheus  │──────────────────→│  Long-Term Store │             │
│  │ (30d local) │  every sample     │  (years)         │             │
│  │             │  Snappy+protobuf  │  Thanos/Cortex/  │             │
│  └─────────────┘                   │  Mimir/Victoria  │             │
│                                    └──────────────────┘             │
│                                                                      │
│  REMOTE READ (Prometheus ← External Storage):                        │
│                                                                      │
│  ┌─────────────┐   remote_read     ┌──────────────────┐             │
│  │ Prometheus  │←──────────────────│  Long-Term Store │             │
│  │ (queries    │  PromQL → remote  │  (historical     │             │
│  │  data)      │  data returned    │   data)          │             │
│  └─────────────┘                   └──────────────────┘             │
│                                                                      │
│  Configuration (prometheus.yml):                                      │
│  remote_write:                                                        │
│  - url: "http://thanos-receive:19291/api/v1/receive"                 │
│    remote_timeout: 30s                                               │
│    queue_config:                                                      │
│      capacity: 2500          # samples per shard                     │
│      max_shards: 200         # max parallel shards                   │
│      max_samples_per_send: 500                                       │
│                                                                      │
│  💡 Remote write: for LONG-TERM storage beyond local retention        │
│  💡 Remote read: for querying historical data transparently           │
│  💡 Both use Snappy-compressed protobuf over HTTP                    │

└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Remote write/read are Prometheus fundamentals. Know that remote write forwards samples to external storage, remote read queries external storage, and both are used for long-term retention beyond Prometheus's local TSDB limits.

What does the Prometheus Operator automate that you would otherwise do manually when running Prometheus on Kubernetes?
A) It watches ServiceMonitor/PodMonitor CRDs and automatically generates scrape_configs, reloads Prometheus config, manages Prometheus StatefulSets, and handles Rule updates — replacing manual prometheus.yml ConfigMap editing and POST /-/reload
B) It automatically writes PromQL queries for you based on your application code
C) It replaces kubectl — you manage Prometheus through the Operator CLI instead of kubectl
D) It automatically scales Prometheus pods based on CPU usage — it's a Kubernetes HPA for Prometheus
✅ Correct Answer: A

The Prometheus Operator automates the entire Prometheus-on-Kubernetes lifecycle: (1) You create a ServiceMonitor CRD saying "scrape Services with app=anihpj-api on port 8000." (2) The Operator watches this CRD and automatically generates the corresponding scrape_config section. (3) It updates the Prometheus configuration Secret/ConfigMap. (4) It triggers a config reload (POST /-/reload or lifecycle hook). (5) It manages the Prometheus StatefulSet (replicas, PVC, resources). (6) When you update PrometheusRule CRDs, it syncs them. Without the Operator, you'd manually edit ConfigMaps, reload config, and manage StatefulSets — error-prone at scale.

│  4. Operator UPDATES config + RELOADS Prometheus                 │
│  5. Done — Prometheus scrapes new service                        │
│  ✅ Declarative, automatic, GitOps-ready, scales infinitely      │

Fundamentals Q54 of 209
What are the top-level sections of a prometheus.yml configuration file, and what goes in each?
A) Only scrape_configs — all other settings are command-line flags only
B) inputs, outputs, filters, and processors — Prometheus uses a data pipeline model
C) global (defaults for scrape_interval, evaluation_interval, external_labels), scrape_configs (job definitions with SD, relabeling, auth), rule_files (paths to recording/alerting rule YAML files), alerting (Alertmanager targets). These 4 sections define the complete Prometheus behavior
D) server, storage, security, and network — Prometheus config is organized by subsystem
✅ Correct Answer: C

A prometheus.yml has 4 top-level sections: global — default scrape_interval (15s), evaluation_interval (15s), external_labels (cluster name, region). scrape_configs — one per job, each with job_name, scrape_interval, service discovery (kubernetes_sd_configs, static_configs), relabel_configs, metric_relabel_configs, auth settings. rule_files — paths to YAML files containing recording rules and alerting rules (e.g., "rules/*.yml"). alerting — Alertmanager targets with scheme, timeout, auth. For anihpj: global sets region=us-east-1, scrape_configs define how to scrape Django pods via K8s SD, rule_files point to anihpj-alerts.yml.

│  global:                                                          │
│    scrape_interval: 15s       # default for all jobs              │
│    evaluation_interval: 15s   # how often to evaluate rules       │
│    external_labels:                                               │
│      cluster: 'anihpj-prod'                                       │
│      region: 'us-east-1'                                          │

│      - targets: ['alertmanager:9093']                             │
│                                                                  │

│    - job_name: 'anihpj-api'                                       │
│      scrape_interval: 30s                                         │
│      kubernetes_sd_configs:                                       │
│      - role: pod                                                  │
│      relabel_configs: ...                                         │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 global = defaults (can be overridden per job)                │

Fundamentals Q55 of 209
What is scrape_interval, and what happens if you set it too low or too high for anihpj/jobpost?
A) scrape_interval controls how often alerts fire — lower means faster alerting, higher means slower
B) scrape_interval = how often Prometheus scrapes each target (default 15s). Too low (1s): excessive load on targets, Prometheus CPU/memory spikes, TSDB bloat. Too high (5min): missed spikes, stale data, delayed alerts. For anihpj API: 15-30s is ideal — catches latency spikes without overloading Django
C) scrape_interval is always 15 seconds and cannot be changed — it's hardcoded in Prometheus
D) scrape_interval controls how long Prometheus retains data — lower means less retention, higher means more
✅ Correct Answer: B

scrape_interval (default 15s) is the frequency at which Prometheus scrapes targets. It's a critical tuning parameter: Too low (1-5s) — Prometheus hammers targets with HTTP requests, increasing CPU on both sides, and the TSDB stores 15× more samples (15s→1s = 15× storage). Too high (5min) — Prometheus may miss brief spikes (a 2-minute latency spike between scrapes is invisible), alerts are delayed up to 5 minutes, and data resolution is coarse. For anihpj: web/api at 15-30s (request-driven, need responsiveness), node_exporter at 30-60s (infrastructure changes slowly), RDS postgres_exporter at 60s (database metrics change gradually). Rule of thumb: scrape_interval should be less than 1/4 of your alert's `for` duration.

📝 Detailed Explanation
scrape_interval Tradeoffs — Finding the Sweet Spot
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  SCRAPE INTERVAL     PROS                    CONS                │
│  ───────────────     ────────────────────    ──────────────────  │
│  1-5s (aggressive)   ✅ Catch every spike    ❌ High CPU/memory   │
│                       ✅ Fast alerts          ❌ TSDB bloat (15×)  │
│                       ✅ High-res graphs      ❌ Target overload    │
│                                                                  │
│  15-30s (default)    ✅ Good balance          ✅ Recommended       │
│   ← anihpj API        ✅ Catches most issues   ✅ Manageable load  │
│                                                                  │
│  60-120s (relaxed)   ✅ Low resource usage    ❌ Miss brief spikes │
│                       ✅ Good for infra        ❌ Slow alerts       │
│   ← node_exporter     ✅ Stable metrics        ❌ Coarse graphs    │
│                                                                  │
│  ⚡ ALERTING RULE: for: duration should be ≥ 4× scrape_interval  │
│    scrape_interval=15s → for: 1m minimum to avoid flapping       │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 Per-job override: scrape_interval in scrape_config           │
│  💡 Global default: global.scrape_interval in prometheus.yml    │
│  💡 scrape_timeout MUST be < scrape_interval (default 10s < 15s) │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: The PCA exam tests scrape_interval understanding — know the tradeoffs, defaults, and per-job override syntax. Remember: scrape_timeout < scrape_interval always.

Fundamentals Q56 of 209
What is the difference between scrape_timeout and scrape_interval, and what rule must they follow?
A) scrape_interval = how often to scrape; scrape_timeout = max time to wait for a single scrape response. Rule: scrape_timeout MUST be less than scrape_interval (default: 10s timeout < 15s interval) — otherwise a slow target would overlap with the next scrape cycle
B) They are the same thing — scrape_timeout is just an alias for scrape_interval
C) scrape_timeout should be LONGER than scrape_interval to ensure all data is collected
D) scrape_interval controls target discovery; scrape_timeout controls data retention
✅ Correct Answer: A

scrape_interval (default 15s) is the time BETWEEN scrape starts. scrape_timeout (default 10s) is the maximum time Prometheus waits for a single HTTP GET /metrics to complete. The critical rule: scrape_timeout < scrape_interval. If timeout ≥ interval, a slow target would still be responding when the NEXT scrape cycle begins — causing overlapping scrapes, resource contention, and potentially missed data. For anihpj: if the Django /metrics endpoint takes 8s (maybe a slow DB query in middleware), the default 10s timeout is fine. If it regularly exceeds 15s, you need to fix the endpoint (not increase timeout).

📝 Detailed Explanation
scrape_interval vs scrape_timeout — Must NOT Overlap
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  CORRECT (timeout < interval):                                    │
│                                                                  │
│  t=0s    t=10s   t=15s        t=25s   t=30s                     │
│  ├────────┤       ├────────────┤                                │
│  │ SCRAPE │ idle  │  SCRAPE    │ idle                            │
│  │ (10s)  │       │  (10s)     │                                │
│  └────────┘       └────────────┘                                │
│  ← 15s interval → ← 15s interval →                              │
│  ✅ No overlap — clean scrape cycles                             │
│                                                                  │
│  WRONG (timeout > interval):                                      │
│                                                                  │
│  t=0s         t=15s    t=20s                                     │
│  ├─────────────┤                                                 │
│  │  SCRAPE (20s timeout)  │                                      │
│  └─────────────────────────┤                                     │
│              ├─────────────┤                                     │
│              │  SCRAPE #2 starts while #1 still running!         │
│              └─────────────┘                                     │
│  ❌ Overlapping scrapes — resource contention, data issues       │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 Default: scrape_timeout=10s, scrape_interval=15s             │
│  💡 Rule: timeout < interval — always                            │
│  💡 If target is slow: fix the target, don't increase timeout    │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: The PCA exam tests the relationship between scrape_interval and scrape_timeout. Know the rule: timeout < interval, and the defaults (10s < 15s).

Fundamentals Q57 of 209
What is evaluation_interval, and how does it differ from scrape_interval?
A) evaluation_interval controls how often targets are discovered — it's the service discovery refresh rate
B) evaluation_interval is the same as scrape_interval — Prometheus evaluates rules and scrapes targets at the same frequency
C) evaluation_interval controls how often Prometheus evaluates recording rules and alerting rules (default 15s). It's independent from scrape_interval — rules are evaluated against the latest scraped data, not during scraping. You can evaluate rules faster than you scrape for more responsive alerting
D) evaluation_interval controls how often Prometheus compacts TSDB blocks — it's a storage setting
✅ Correct Answer: C

evaluation_interval (default 15s) is how often Prometheus evaluates recording and alerting rules against the latest data in the TSDB. It's independent from scrape_interval: Prometheus doesn't wait for a scrape to finish before evaluating rules — rules run on their own schedule against whatever data is currently in the TSDB. For anihpj: we scrape every 15s but evaluate alerting rules every 30s (less CPU for complex PromQL alerts). Recording rules might evaluate every 15s for up-to-date precomputed metrics. Key insight: if evaluation_interval > scrape_interval, alerts are slightly delayed but use less CPU — acceptable for most scenarios.

📝 Detailed Explanation
evaluation_interval vs scrape_interval — Independent Timers
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  TIMELINE:                                                         │
│                                                                  │
│  Scrapes (every 15s):                                             │
│  t=0s    t=15s    t=30s    t=45s    t=60s                        │
│  ├────────┤────────┤────────┤────────┤                           │
│  │SCRAPE  │SCRAPE  │SCRAPE  │SCRAPE  │  ← new data arrives       │
│  └────────┴────────┴────────┴────────┘                           │
│                                                                  │
│  Rule Evaluation (every 15s):                                     │
│  t=0s    t=15s    t=30s    t=45s    t=60s                        │
│  ├────────┤────────┤────────┤────────┤                           │
│  │EVAL    │EVAL    │EVAL    │EVAL    │  ← rules check TSDB       │
│  └────────┴────────┴────────┴────────┘                           │
│  → Alert fires at t=15s if scrape at t=0s shows problem         │
│                                                                  │
│  Rule Evaluation (every 30s — more relaxed):                      │
│  t=0s              t=30s              t=60s                      │
│  ├──────────────────┤──────────────────┤                         │
│  │      EVAL        │      EVAL        │                         │
│  └──────────────────┴──────────────────┘                         │
│  → Alert fires at t=30s (15s delay vs 15s eval_interval)        │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 scrape_interval = "how fresh is the data?"                   │
│  💡 evaluation_interval = "how fast do alerts react?"            │
│  💡 They run independently — rules evaluate against DB, not live │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Know that evaluation_interval is for rules (not scraping), it runs independently from scrape_interval, and it can be tuned per rule group for performance vs responsiveness.

Fundamentals Q58 of 209
How does Prometheus scrape anihpj/jobpost — what exactly happens during an HTTP GET to /metrics?
A) Prometheus executes a SQL query against the application database to extract metrics
B) Prometheus reads /var/log/anihpj.log and parses log entries into metrics using regex patterns
C) The application pushes metrics to Prometheus via a WebSocket connection — /metrics is just a status page
D) Prometheus sends HTTP GET to http://<pod-ip>:8000/metrics → Django's django-prometheus middleware intercepts → collects current Counter/Gauge/Histogram values → formats them in Prometheus exposition format (HELP, TYPE, metric{labels} value) → returns text/plain response → Prometheus parses text into time series samples → appends to TSDB via WAL
✅ Correct Answer: D

The scrape process for anihpj: (1) Prometheus resolves the target (pod IP:8000 via K8s SD). (2) HTTP GET to /metrics. (3) Django's django-prometheus middleware intercepts the request. (4) The prometheus_client library collects current metric values — Counters (http_requests_total), Gauges (active_users), Histograms (api_request_duration_seconds). (5) Formats them as text: # HELP http_requests_total Total HTTP requests, # TYPE http_requests_total counter, http_requests_total{method="GET",status="200"} 54321. (6) Returns text/plain. (7) Prometheus parses this text into structured time series samples. (8) Appends samples to WAL (durability), then to head block (queryable). Done.

Fundamentals Q59 of 209
What is metrics_path in a scrape_config, and when would you change it from the default /metrics?
A) metrics_path is the filesystem path where Prometheus stores TSDB data — change it to use a different disk
B) metrics_path is the URL path Prometheus requests on each target (default: /metrics). Change it when an exporter exposes metrics at a non-standard path — e.g., Jenkins exporter at /prometheus, Spring Boot at /actuator/prometheus, or a custom app at /custom-metrics
C) metrics_path is the path to the prometheus.yml configuration file on disk — change it with --config.file flag
D) metrics_path is always /metrics — it cannot be changed
✅ Correct Answer: B

metrics_path (default: /metrics) specifies which URL path Prometheus requests when scraping a target. Most exporters and client libraries serve metrics at /metrics by convention, but some use different paths: Jenkins Prometheus plugin → /prometheus, Spring Boot Actuator → /actuator/prometheus, Envoy → /stats/prometheus, custom apps might use /custom-metrics. For anihpj: django-prometheus serves at the standard /metrics, so the default works. Always check the exporter documentation for the correct metrics_path — mismatched path = scrape failure (target shows DOWN).

Fundamentals Q60 of 209
How do you configure Prometheus to scrape HTTPS targets with TLS, and what happens if you use scheme: https without TLS config?
A) Set scheme: https in scrape_config and provide TLS settings (ca_file, cert_file, key_file, insecure_skip_verify). Without TLS config, Prometheus attempts HTTPS with system CA pool — if the target uses a self-signed cert, the scrape fails with "x509: certificate signed by unknown authority"
B) Prometheus automatically negotiates HTTPS — no configuration needed. scheme: https is just for display
C) HTTPS scraping requires a separate proxy like nginx — Prometheus can only scrape HTTP targets
D) Set scheme: https and Prometheus generates a self-signed certificate automatically for each target
✅ Correct Answer: A

To scrape HTTPS targets, set scheme: https in the scrape_config. By default, Prometheus verifies TLS certificates against the system CA pool. For self-signed or internal CA certificates, provide ca_file (custom CA). For mutual TLS (mTLS), also provide cert_file and key_file. For testing only, insecure_skip_verify: true skips verification (⚠️ insecure!). For anihpj in production: Prometheus scrapes Django via HTTP internally (within the cluster), but scrapes external RDS postgres_exporter via HTTPS with a proper CA-signed certificate.

📝 Detailed Explanation
TLS Scrape Configuration
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  scrape_configs:                                                   │
│    - job_name: 'secure-service'                                    │
│      scheme: https                                                 │
│      tls_config:                                                   │
│        ca_file: /etc/prometheus/certs/ca.crt     # for custom CA  │
│        cert_file: /etc/prometheus/certs/client.crt # for mTLS     │
│        key_file: /etc/prometheus/certs/client.key  # for mTLS     │
│        insecure_skip_verify: false              # NEVER true      │
│        server_name: 'service.anihpj.com'       # SNI override    │
│                                                                  │
│  TLS VERIFICATION FLOW:                                           │
│    Prometheus → TCP connect → TLS handshake → verify cert chain  │
│    → if valid: GET /metrics → if invalid: "x509: unknown CA"     │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 scheme: https REQUIRES valid TLS config or scrape fails      │
│  💡 insecure_skip_verify: true = "I don't care about security"   │
│  💡 For K8s internal: use HTTP (pod network is trusted)          │
│  💡 For external: use HTTPS with proper certificates             │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: TLS configuration for scraping is a PCA topic. Know the tls_config fields (ca_file, cert_file, key_file, insecure_skip_verify) and the security implications.

Fundamentals Q61 of 209
How do you configure authentication for scraping protected /metrics endpoints that require a username/password or bearer token?
A) Authentication is not supported — Prometheus can only scrape unauthenticated endpoints
B) Put credentials in the URL: https://user:pass@target:9090/metrics — Prometheus automatically extracts them
C) Use environment variables PROMETHEUS_USERNAME and PROMETHEUS_PASSWORD — Prometheus reads them automatically
D) Use basic_auth (username/password or username_file/password_file for secrets) or bearer_token/bearer_token_file in the scrape_config. For OAuth2, use the oauth2 block. Credentials are sent as HTTP headers with each scrape request
✅ Correct Answer: D

Prometheus supports multiple authentication methods in scrape_configs: basic_auth with username/password (or username_file/password_file for Kubernetes Secrets), bearer_token or bearer_token_file (for JWT/API token auth), and oauth2 block for OAuth2 client credentials flow. For anihpj: internal pod scraping uses no auth (trusted network), but if we exposed /metrics externally or used a service mesh with mTLS, we'd configure bearer_token_file pointing to a Kubernetes Secret-mounted token. Best practice: use _file variants to avoid putting secrets directly in prometheus.yml.

│                                                                  │

Why this matters for the PCA exam: The PCA exam tests scrape authentication configuration. Know basic_auth, bearer_token, oauth2, and that _file variants are preferred for secrets.

Fundamentals Q62 of 209
What do honor_labels and honor_timestamps do in a scrape_config?
A) honor_labels makes Prometheus respect the target's labels; honor_timestamps makes Prometheus respect the target's scrape_interval
B) honor_labels (default false): if true, target's labels override Prometheus's labels when they conflict (e.g., target says job="custom" → keeps it instead of scrape_config's job_name). honor_timestamps (default true): if true, Prometheus uses the timestamp from the target's /metrics response; if false, Prometheus uses its own scrape timestamp
C) Both are deprecated and no longer used in Prometheus 2.x
D) honor_labels controls whether to add labels; honor_timestamps controls whether to add timestamps
✅ Correct Answer: B

honor_labels (default: false): When a target's /metrics already includes a label that Prometheus would normally set (like job or instance), should the target's value win? false = Prometheus overrides (safe default). true = target's label wins (useful for federation where the downstream Prometheus already has meaningful job/instance labels). honor_timestamps (default: true): Should Prometheus use the timestamp provided in the /metrics exposition format, or its own scrape timestamp? true = trust the target (default, good for accurate event timing). false = use Prometheus's scrape time (useful when target clocks are unreliable).

📝 Detailed Explanation
honor_labels vs honor_timestamps
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  honor_labels: false (default)                                     │
│    Target returns: metric{job="custom",instance="myapp"} 42       │
│    Prometheus overrides: job → scrape_config job_name             │
│                          instance → target host:port              │
│    Final: metric{job="anihpj-api",instance="10.244.1.23:8000"} 42│
│                                                                  │
│  honor_labels: true                                                │
│    Target returns: metric{job="custom",instance="myapp"} 42       │
│    Prometheus respects target labels: job="custom" stays          │
│    Final: metric{job="custom",instance="myapp"} 42                │
│    ⚠️ Can cause label conflicts — use carefully!                  │
│                                                                  │
│  honor_timestamps: true (default)                                  │
│    Target returns: metric{...} 42 1718387535                      │
│    Prometheus uses: 1718387535 (target's timestamp)               │
│    ✅ Good: accurate event timing                                 │
│                                                                  │
│  honor_timestamps: false                                           │
│    Target returns: metric{...} 42 1718387535                      │
│    Prometheus ignores target timestamp                            │
│    Prometheus uses: own scrape timestamp (e.g., 1718387550)       │
│    ✅ Good: when target clocks are unreliable                     │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 honor_labels=true: useful for federation                      │
│  💡 honor_timestamps=false: useful for unreliable target clocks   │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: honor_labels and honor_timestamps are scrape_config fields tested on the PCA. Know their defaults and when to change them (federation for honor_labels, unreliable clocks for honor_timestamps).

Fundamentals Q63 of 209
How do you reload Prometheus configuration without restarting the server, and what flag must be enabled?
A) Send HTTP POST to /-/reload (or SIGHUP signal to the process). Requires Prometheus to be started with --web.enable-lifecycle flag. This hot-reloads prometheus.yml, rule files, and target definitions without dropping in-flight scrapes or queries
B) Run prometheus --reload from the command line — it sends a reload signal to the running process
C) Edit prometheus.yml and Prometheus detects the file change automatically via inotify within 15 seconds
D) Prometheus cannot be reloaded — you must restart the process for any config change
✅ Correct Answer: A

Prometheus supports hot-reloading via POST /-/reload or sending SIGHUP (kill -HUP) to the process. The --web.enable-lifecycle flag MUST be set at startup — without it, /-/reload returns 404. On reload, Prometheus re-reads prometheus.yml, rule files, and target definitions, applying changes without restarting. In-flight scrapes and queries continue uninterrupted. For anihpj on Kubernetes: the Prometheus Operator automatically POSTs to /-/reload when ServiceMonitor CRDs change, making config updates seamless. Manual reload: curl -X POST http://prometheus:9090/-/reload.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Prometheus Config Reload — Hot Reload Flow
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  METHOD 1: HTTP POST /-/reload                                     │
│                                                                  │
│  $ curl -X POST http://localhost:9090/-/reload                    │
│  → Prometheus re-reads prometheus.yml                             │
│  → Prometheus re-reads rule files                                 │
│  → Prometheus updates scrape targets                              │
│  → Response: 200 OK (or 404 if --web.enable-lifecycle not set)    │
│                                                                  │
│  METHOD 2: SIGHUP signal                                           │
│                                                                  │
│  $ kill -HUP $(pidof prometheus)                                   │
│  → Same effect as POST /-/reload                                  │
│  → Useful in non-containerized environments                      │
│                                                                  │
│  WHAT GETS RELOADED:                                               │
│    ✅ prometheus.yml (scrape configs, global settings)            │
│    ✅ Rule files (recording rules, alerting rules)                │
│    ✅ Target definitions (new targets discovered, old removed)    │
│                                                                  │
│  WHAT DOES NOT GET RELOADED:                                       │
│    ❌ Command-line flags (--storage.tsdb.path, etc.) — restart req│
│    ❌ Remote write/read endpoints — requires restart              │
│                                                                  │
│  💡 --web.enable-lifecycle MUST be set (not default!)             │
│  💡 SIGHUP is the traditional Unix way; /-/reload is HTTP-native │
│  💡 Prometheus Operator does this automatically via CRD changes  │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this matters for the PCA exam: Config reload is a fundamental Prometheus operation. Know: POST /-/reload (or SIGHUP), --web.enable-lifecycle must be enabled, and what gets reloaded vs what requires a restart.

2.3 Understanding LimitationsQ64-Q71

Fundamentals Q64 of 209
What is Prometheus NOT designed for, and what tools should you use instead for those use cases?
A) Prometheus is a complete observability platform — it handles metrics, logs, traces, and dashboards natively
B) Prometheus only lacks authentication — everything else is built-in and production-ready at any scale
C) Prometheus is NOT designed for: long-term storage (use Thanos/Cortex/Mimir), high availability by itself (run 2+ instances), logs (use Loki), traces (use Tempo), dashboarding (use Grafana), horizontal scaling (use federation or Thanos), authentication (use reverse proxy/oauth2-proxy). Prometheus excels at real-time metrics collection + short-term storage + alerting — it's purpose-built, not all-purpose
D) Prometheus is designed to replace Elasticsearch, Grafana, and Jaeger — it's an all-in-one platform
✅ Correct Answer: C

Prometheus is purpose-built, not all-purpose. It excels at: real-time metrics scraping, short-term TSDB storage (days to weeks), PromQL querying, and alerting rule evaluation.
